ACADEMY · 4 courses live · 8 coming · one reader

Academy

Every self-study course in one place, grouped by track. Each course is written one unit at a time to the same method and the same sourcing standard, audited every four units, and read in order. Pick up where you left off, or open a track.

History

The history and mythology of one culture at a time, read together: who told each story, what they gained by it, and what the evidence says. Long sourced reports with disputes named, a self-test and a syllabus you can rerun.

Why history and myth together

the method of the track
μῦθοςmythos

In Homer, simply what someone says with authority: a speech, a command, a tale. By the fifth century it had come to mean the story you cannot check.

λόγοςlogos

A word, a count, an account. By the same century it meant the argued explanation, the one that gives its reasons and invites you to test them.

The Greeks drew that line themselves. Herodotus calls his work a historiē, an inquiry, and still reports what the Persians and Egyptians told him. Thucydides warns that his history leaves out the mythōdes, the storylike, and will seem less pleasant for it (1.22). Plato expels the poets from his ideal city and then ends the Republic with a myth of his own.

The tidy story of a clean progress "from mythos to logos" (the title of Wilhelm Nestle's book of 1940) is now itself treated as a modern myth: the Greeks never stopped telling myths; their philosophers used them, their historians argued with them, and their cities were governed by them. So each course reads a culture's myths and its history together, asks who told each story and what they gained by it, and keeps the evidence in view. The aim is not to debunk the myths but to understand them as well as the history. Later courses point back to earlier ones where they touch: Rome to Greece's units 7 and 10, Persia to units 6 and 11, Egypt to units 1 and 7.

Coming next, in the intended order: the two civilisations that border the Greek story, then the northern and insular traditions, then East Asia. Open any course to see its planned scope.

EgyptEgyptian history and mythologycoming soon

Three thousand years, thirty-one dynasties, and a religion the Greeks themselves treated as the oldest in the world. Predynastic unification, the Old Kingdom and the pyramids, the Middle and New Kingdoms, Akhenaten, Ramesses and the Sea Peoples, the Late Period and Persian rule, the Ptolemies you met in unit 7; then the cosmogonies of Heliopolis, Memphis and Hermopolis, the Osiris cycle, the solar journey, ma'at, the Book of the Dead and the afterlife, temple and priesthood, and how to read hieroglyphs enough to recognise a cartouche.

SpinePyramid Texts · Coffin Texts · Book of the Dead · the Shabaka Stone · Herodotus book 2 · Wilkinson, The Rise and Fall of Ancient Egypt · Assmann, The Search for God in Ancient Egypt

PersiaPersian history and mythologycoming soon

The other side of units 6 and 11, told from Persepolis rather than Athens. Elam and the Medes, Cyrus and the Achaemenid empire, Darius and Xerxes as the Persians saw them, the satrapal system, Zoroaster and the Avesta, Ahura Mazda and the dualist cosmos, the Parthians, the Sasanians and their wars with Rome, the Zoroastrian apocalyptic, then the Shahnameh as the national epic that fused myth and history into one narrative of kings.

SpineBehistun inscription · the Avesta (Gathas, Yashts) · Herodotus · Xenophon, Cyropaedia · Firdausi, Shahnameh · Briant, From Cyrus to Alexander · Boyce, Zoroastrians

NorseViking and Norse history and mythologycoming soon

The Scandinavian Iron Age and the Viking expansion, 793 to 1066 and beyond: raids, trade routes to Byzantium and Baghdad, the settlement of Iceland, Greenland and Vinland, the Danelaw, Normandy, the Rus, the conversion. Then the mythology as the two Eddas preserve it: Ymir and the world's making, Yggdrasil and the nine worlds, Odin, Thor, Loki, Freyja and the Vanir war, the death of Baldr, Ragnarok, the heroic legends of Sigurd and the Volsungs, and the problem of a pagan mythology written down by Christians two centuries late.

SpinePoetic Edda · Snorri, Prose Edda · Heimskringla · Saga of the Volsungs · Egil's Saga · Njal's Saga · Price, Children of Ash and Elm · Lindow, Norse Mythology

CeltsCeltic history and mythologycoming soon

Hallstatt and La Tène, the Celtic expansion across Europe, the sack of Rome and Delphi, Gaul and Caesar's conquest, the druids as the Greeks and Romans described them, Roman Britain, then the two literary survivals: the Irish cycles (the Mythological Cycle and the Tuatha Dé Danann, the Ulster Cycle and Cú Chulainn, the Fenian Cycle, the Kings' Cycle) and the Welsh Mabinogion, with the question of how much is pre-Christian myth and how much medieval literature.

SpineCaesar, Gallic War · Lebor Gabála Érenn · Táin Bó Cúailnge · the Mabinogion · Cunliffe, The Ancient Celts · Koch, Celtic Culture

EnglandEnglish history and mythologycoming soon

From Roman Britain and the Anglo-Saxon settlement through Alfred, the Conquest, the Angevins, Magna Carta, the Wars of the Roses, the Tudors and the Reformation, the Civil War, and the making of the constitutional state. Alongside it the legendary matter: Beowulf and the Germanic inheritance, Bede's England, Geoffrey of Monmouth's invented history and the Arthurian cycle from Nennius to Malory, Robin Hood, the Green Man, and how England manufactured a national past.

SpineBeowulf · Bede · the Anglo-Saxon Chronicle · Geoffrey of Monmouth · Malory, Le Morte d'Arthur · Tombs, The English and Their History · Morris, The Anglo-Saxons

ScotlandScottish history and mythologycoming soon

Picts, Scots, Britons and Angles; Dál Riata and the kingdom of Alba; Macbeth as he was; the Wars of Independence, Wallace and Bruce; the Stewarts, the Reformation and Knox, the Union of Crowns and of Parliaments, the Jacobite risings, the Clearances, the Enlightenment. Then the mythology and legend: the Pictish stones, the Celtic saints (Columba, Iona), the Fenian material in Gaelic Scotland, the kelpies, selkies and the Sìth, the Border ballads, Ossian and the eighteenth-century invention of Highland tradition.

SpineAdomnán, Life of Columba · the Declaration of Arbroath · Barbour, The Brus · the Child Ballads · Macpherson's Ossian · Devine, The Scottish Nation · Fraser, From Caledonia to Pictland

ChinaChinese history and mythologycoming soon

The Shang and the oracle bones, the Zhou and the Mandate of Heaven, the Warring States and the hundred schools, Qin unification, the Han, the period of division, Tang, Song, Yuan, Ming and Qing down to 1911. Then the mythology, which China kept largely in philosophy and ritual rather than epic: Pangu and Nüwa, the Three Sovereigns and Five Emperors, Yu and the flood, the Queen Mother of the West, the Jade Emperor and the bureaucratic heaven, Daoist immortals, Buddhist arrivals, and the great vernacular novels as a late mythology (Journey to the West, Investiture of the Gods).

SpineShijing · Shangshu · Sima Qian, Records of the Grand Historian · Shanhaijing · Zhuangzi · Journey to the West · Lewis, The Early Chinese Empires · Birrell, Chinese Mythology

JapanJapanese history and mythologycoming soon

Jōmon and Yayoi, the Yamato state and the Chinese model, Nara and Heian court culture, the rise of the warriors, Kamakura, the Mongol invasions, the Sengoku wars and unification, Tokugawa isolation, Meiji. Then the mythology as the Kojiki and Nihon Shoki set it down to legitimise the imperial house: Izanagi and Izanami, Amaterasu and the cave, Susanoo and the serpent, the descent of Ninigi, Jimmu; Shinto as practice, kami and shrines, the Buddhist synthesis, the yōkai tradition, and the Tale of the Heike as a national epic of defeat.

SpineKojiki · Nihon Shoki · Man'yōshū · Tale of Genji · Tale of the Heike · Totman, A History of Japan · Breen and Teeuwen, A New History of Shinto

How every course works

the constants
  1. One reader. Every course is written for the same person and read in order, on a phone, with the citations checked.
  2. Sources first. A unit names its sources and says where they disagree; a claim that cannot be sourced is marked unsourced or left out.
  3. One unit per chat, built in full. Each unit is written from a published brief or prompt, in its own chat, with its closing artefact: a self-test and carry-forward ideas for the history courses, a shipped deliverable for Brand + AI.
  4. Audited every four units. After every four units written in a hub, a stratified sample of claims is checked against its sources with a second model; a unit that fails gets a full audit. The model that wrote each unit is recorded.
  5. An open syllabus. Every prompt and brief is published with its course, so any unit can be regenerated, extended or checked.

CHANGELOG · 19 sessions since 22 October 2026

Changelog

Everything that has happened to Personal Mythology and, from session 13, to Academy, from the first request on 22 October 2026 to now, newest first. Each entry is one working session. It is assembled from the kit's own records (provenance.json, the review folders and the maintainer log) and from the session transcripts. Where a session recorded the site's version number, it is given. The exact day of each step in the first, three-day session was not recorded; its entries are in order.

Where things stand

  • Greece, course 1: complete. 78 reports, about 244,000 words: 55 written with Fable 5.1, 23 with Opus 5.5.
  • Rome, course 2: 59 of 79 reports, about 180,000 words, all written with Opus 5.5 and all checked. 20 modules are queued: deep dive I (11.3 to 11.8), deep dive II, the Aeneid (12.1 to 12.8), and deep dive III (13.3 to 13.8).
  • Fable review: every Rome report is reviewed (session 9). Of the 23 Greek Opus reports, 17 are reviewed (eleven audited in full, six cleared on the sample) and six are still flagged: 11.1, 11.2, 11.4, 11.5, 11.6, 11.8 (session 10). Rome 14.3 and the Greek 13.3 high-signal pairs are done; every pair passed.
  • Bibliography: every report has a machine-extracted unit list and a curated r-<id>.md (session 10; the curated files are flagged until Fable spot-checks three units).
  • Corrections: all nine that were waiting (Greek 6.3, 7.2, 7.3, 8.1, 9.3, 9.4; Rome 1.4, 3.1, 6.1) were applied on 27 October. None is waiting.
  • Theme and home (sessions 11 and 12): shell.css is a copy of the Learning Project's design/theme.css. The kit lives in the private repo apcanario/learning as kit/; the zip is retired. The only published page is the site; the standalone syllabus artifacts and the stray draft pages are deleted.
  • A2 (session 19): the site is a shell (747 KB) plus one file per report (143), fetched when a report is opened; personal-mythology.html is retired and the build is academy/. Site version 55.
  • A3 (session 18): the Brand + AI courses are read inside Academy (mdcourse.py), each with its units, briefs, build order, audits, bibliography and changelog; the standalone hub is retired. Backlog page, collapsible drawer groups, theme v3 hover states. Site version 50.
  • DD-B3 (session 17): course B is 8 core units and 3 deep dives; Academy shows B 2 of 11. Site version 49.
  • Navigable (session 16): breadcrumbs on every page, Start or Continue on every course page; course B is Using AI to Build a Brand. Site version 47.
  • Catalogue (session 15): the home lists every course under its track (History, Marketing, Technology); Brand + AI is two Academy courses, A and B. Site version 46.
  • Responsive (session 14): design/theme.css version 2 adds section 6: tablet from 768 px (760 px column, cards in two columns), desktop from 1100 px (the drawer is a persistent 300 px sidebar). The phone layout is unchanged. verify.py runs a phone, tablet and desktop pass. Publishing Academy means the artifact, then ./deploy.sh.
  • Academy (session 13): the site is Academy: its home lists every course of both hubs, Brand + AI as a placeholder panel until A3. deploy.sh publishes the built page to GitHub Pages through the public repo apcanario/academy (https://apcanario.github.io/academy/).

Sessions, newest first

28 October 2026Session 19The split: a shell plus one file per report (A2)

Backlog row A2 of docs/academy.md, Opus 5.5. The single page was 4.9 MB against a 16 MB cap with eight history courses still to come. No report changed and no flag was cleared.

The build

build.py writes academy/: index.html, the shell (home, drawer, course homes, syllabi, build pages, changelog, backlog), and one file per report, academy/<course>/r-<id>.html for Greece and Rome and academy/brand-<k>/read-<code>.html for a Brand + AI reading page. A report panel stays in the shell as an empty stub carrying its attributes and data-src; the file is the panel's inner HTML. The shell now declares UTF-8 itself. personal-mythology.html is deleted. Two builds in a row give the same files.

The shell

shell.js fetches a report the first time it is opened, keeps it in the panel for the rest of the visit, builds its pager once it has landed, and fetches the next report in the course in the background. Clicks on data-target links and copy buttons are delegated, so fetched content behaves like content that was in the page. A section link into an unopened report (#m23-2) loads the report, then scrolls to the section. A failed fetch shows the report's title, the reason and a Try again button. Hash routing, prev/next, Continue and stored progress are unchanged.

Checks

verify.py serves academy/ over local HTTP, as the artifact and Pages do, and every pass waits for the fetch before measuring a report (P13): phone, light, tablet (which now also measures a Rome report) and desktop. New: the files on disk (every stub has its file, no file is orphaned, no report file carries a style or script, the shell is under 1 MB, README.md's size table matches) and the split in the browser (empty stub until opened, fetched once and kept, the next one prefetched, the pager, a section link on a fresh load, a 503 then the retry). ALL CHECKS PASSED. The published site was also opened in the real artifact viewer and a report fetched and rendered there.

Sizes

Shell 747 KB; Greece 78 files, 2.12 MB; Rome 59, 1.58 MB; course A 4, 218 KB; course B 2, 97 KB; site 4.74 MB. The two syllabi are 284 KB of the shell; each new history course adds about 180 KB to it, so the 1 MB line is about four courses away (P20 proposed).

Publishing

publish_files.py prints the report files as the list the Artifact tool's files takes. Publish = the shell as the page, root academy, that list; then deploy.sh, now site-only. The procedure is in README.md (Site layout and sizes), skills/file-unit, skills/new-course, SETUP.md, the root README, generator/README.md, docs/project-instructions.md, NEXT-SESSION.md and BACKLOG.md. Published as version 55 (144 files), then the shell alone once more after the backlog row was closed (the report files stay on the artifact); Pages pushed after each.

28 October 2026Session 18Brand + AI inside Academy (A3), the Backlog page, collapsible drawer, hover states

Pedro's five points after session 17, worked at once: Brand + AI units opened the hub artifact instead of a page of the site; the public site should show the backlog beside the changelog; the drawer should be collapsible; side-by-side cards should share a height with their buttons on one line; the theme had no hover states. No report changed and no flag was cleared.

A3

mdcourse.py is the markdown course family: build.py reads generator/data/brand-ai/hub.py (unchanged, AD5) and builds one Academy course per course in it (AD6): the course home (goal, outcomes, progress, units in writing order, audits run, course A's Frame), a reading page per written unit (#read-<code>, data-kind="report", so prev/next, Continue and stored progress work), and the "How it is built" pages (#brand-<k>-build, -order, #briefs-<k>, -audits, -bibliography, -changelog). The drawer of each course has two groups: Units (open) and How it is built (closed). No navigation leaves the site. verify.py walks both courses, their reading and build pages, the Continue buttons, the drawer groups and the Backlog page, and runs the panel checks in the light theme as well; it absorbs the retired check_page.py. Retired: generator/build_hub.py, hub.js, check_page.py, build_history.py, hubs/. The skills, SETUP.md, method.md §7 and the READMEs point at Academy.

Backlog page

#backlog (root) renders docs/BACKLOG.md and docs/CHANGELOG.md as they are; the Academy home links it beside the Changelog, and so does the root drawer.

Drawer

Groups are generic (.dunit): a course's units are an accordion (opening one closes the others; the current report's unit opens on arrival), the root tracks are collapsible and open by default, and the reader's choice is remembered per group (hellas-units-open now stores 1 or 0).

Theme version 3

design/theme.css section 7: hover states on cards, rows, drawer items, buttons, chips, summaries and links where a pointer can hover; pressed states everywhere; transitions that respect reduced motion; catalogue cards are flex columns so side-by-side cards share a height with the button on one line (.cgrid stretches). shell.css is the copy.

Published

Version 50 of the site to the Academy artifact and, with it, versions 49 and 50 to GitHub Pages (deploy.sh went through on the third try). The Brand + AI hub and Audience Growth artifacts were deleted on Pedro's word.

Both exclude inline elements (inline code) from the gap measure.

27 October 2026Session 10Bibliographies for every report; the Greek Opus cohort; the overlap checks

Three parts, two models. The curated bibliographies ran on Sonnet 5.5 (agents, Pedro's choice for cost); the review ran on Fable 5.1. No report was written.

Curated bibliographies, all 137 reports

One Sonnet 5.5 agent per unit group (26 groups, about 4.5 million tokens) wrote bibliography/<course>/r-<id>.md for every existing report to bibliography/BIBLIO-BRIEF.md: ancient sources with the passages the report cites, modern works completed to full references, corpora, and a Not resolved list. Web verification was thin (search returned links without text; Crossref blocked), so most modern references completed from memory are marked "(not verified)", "(partly verified)" or "(identified from context)". The files are an artefacts entry in provenance.json, flagged until Fable 5.1 spot-checks about three units. The agents also reported five apparent slips in the reports; three were real and are fixed (Rome 1.5 credited The Genesis of Roman Architecture to three authors, it is Hopkins alone; Greek 1.1 gave Hoeck's Kreta the wrong title and year; Greek 10.2 credited Schliemann of Troy to Calder and Traill, it is Traill), two were not (Rome 6.3's Dobbin 1995 and 10.3's Paul Davies are right; those bibliography files were corrected). Rome 4.2's unverified "(2014)" after William Murray, added in session 9, was removed.

Greek 13.2, 13.5 and 13.4: the Opus cohort

Six-report sample (10.4, 10.9, 11.3, 11.7, 12.1, 12.6), one fresh Fable agent each on the Greek scorecard (audits/AUDIT-BRIEF-GREECE.md): 4.0, 4.5, 4.5, 4.1, 4.8, 4.8. The stratum mean of 4.44 clears the rule, but the two provenances inside the sample differ by half a point (unit 12 with checkers 4.8; units 10 and 11 without, 4.28), the gap sits in one dimension (dispute boxes with unnamed sides, 10.4 and 11.7 at 2 and 3), and a mechanical scan found the same pattern across the unsampled units-10-to-11 reports and almost none in unit 12. The stratum was split (audits/greece-13.4-cohorts.md): unit 12's six unsampled reports cleared "(sampled)"; the eleven of units 10 and 11 audited in full. Done: 10.7 (4.0), 10.8 (4.1), 10.10 (4.25), 10.11 (4.6), 10.12 (3.75, two slips, the lowest score of the review). Every erratum and revise item was applied as exact replacements under 13.5 (named sides for about thirty dispute boxes; titles for about forty modern works; six small factual slips; one new box on Rome's adoption of Aeneas in 10.12), check.py OK on each, and the eleven became "Opus 5.5, reviewed with Fable 5.1". The audits of 11.1, 11.2, 11.4 were cut off by an API spend limit and 11.5, 11.6, 11.8 not started; those six remain flagged.

Rome 14.3 and Greek 13.3: the overlap checks

Mechanical first (audits/overlap.py, seven-word shingles and a name-Jaccard over every written pair the syllabi list: 42 Rome, 38 Greek): verbatim overlap is nil everywhere, so the question was semantic. Readers (one agent per group, audits/OVERLAP-BRIEF.md) then classified the later report section by section for the Rome deep-dive pairs (11.1; 13.1 and 13.2 with their Greek cross-course pairs), the seven unmeasured Rome core pairings and the six Greek pairs with the highest signal: every one of the 22 pairs passed (Rome 90 to 100 percent new; Greek 70 to 95 percent new or deeper), with 4.4 → 10.7 at the threshold. Six contradictions between Greek 6.7 and its deep dives were resolved against 6.7 and fixed (Hephaestion's pyre, Harpalus's talents, P.Oxy. 4808's year, Diodorus's distance, Callisthenes's death, the dismissal of the League allies). The six repeated Greek disputes from the 25 October crosscheck were given one home each with pointers elsewhere (audits/greece-13.3-disputes.md). Three pointers were added (Rome 4.5 → 11.1, Rome 1.5 → 0.3, Greek 6.7 → 11.5 and 11.7). The 32 lower-signal Greek pairs were not read.

27 October 2026Session 9Every Rome report reviewed: the sample, the capstone, the decision

The second session on Fable 5.1. Rome 14.2, 14.5 and 14.4 were run; all 59 Rome reports are off the review queue. No report was written.

The review alert

  • review_due.py was added at Pedro's request: it counts the complete units (both courses pooled) that still hold a flagged report and prints "FABLE REVIEW DUE" at four. register.py and build.py print its line after every registration and build, FABLE-REVIEW.md opens with it, and BACKLOG.md tells a writing session to stop at the unit boundary and say so in its first line when it fires. Tested on simulated states (three units: status line; four: due).

Rome 14.2, twelve reports by stratified sample

  • The 58 unaudited reports were split by how they had been checked: quality-pass "keep" (13), quality-pass "fix" (26) and the fresh-checker pipeline (17), the capstone kept apart. Sample: 0.1, 1.4, 1.6, 4.2, 7.3; 2.3, 3.5, 5.2, 6.4; 8.3, 9.2, 11.2. Each was audited by a fresh agent on the Rome scorecard with the brief in audits/AUDIT-BRIEF.md: whole report read, at least fifteen claims checked (29 to 35 in practice), every pointer opened, every self-test answered, each writer's unsure list and each earlier review's open items taken as the starting point. Scorecards in audits/rome-14.2-r-<id>.md.
  • Scores: 0.1 4.9, 1.4 4.9, 1.6 4.8, 4.2 4.4, 7.3 4.8, 2.3 4.6, 3.5 4.6, 5.2 4.8, 6.4 4.6, 8.3 4.9, 9.2 4.6, 11.2 4.7; mean 4.72. Verdicts 8 pass, 4 revise, 0 rewrite. Four factual slips in 36,459 words: 1.4's "the literature begins about 200 BC"; 2.3's Pliny "thought Cornelia innocent" (he withholds judgement); 4.2's "more than 20,000" mercenaries (Polybius: about 20,000); 11.2's Sulla letter dated 83 (written from the East in 85 or 84). Thirty-seven errata rows in all, the rest citation sharpenings (a title and date for a modern work, a chapter for a book), two dispute boxes given their unnamed side (0.1 Timpe, 4.2 Thiel and Goldsworthy), a loose pointer (3.5 to 4.3), one place-name form (Acragas for Agrigentum) and three self-test answers brought into line with their bodies.
  • 14.5: every erratum and revise item applied as an exact replacement, check.py OK on each (all twelve inside the band), and each entry in provenance.json set to "Opus 5.5, reviewed with Fable 5.1".
  • The 3.5 auditor found the sides reversed in audits/rome-14.6-syllabus.md's coinage row (Burnett and HN Italy hold the early date, Crawford's RRC the late one); corrected there.

Rome 14.4, the decision

  • audits/rome-14.4-cohorts.md. Against the Greek Fable baseline (mean 4.8, spread 4.6 to 4.9) the Rome sample's mean of the eight shared dimensions is 4.68 against 4.78, inside the baseline's own spread: noise. One systematic habit: modern works cited by surname and year with no title (nine of twelve scored 4 on citation for it). Rome's length discipline is better (+12 percent on average, all in band; the Greek core +19 with two out of band). Not found: Livy over-trusted, Greek modules retold, weaker self-tests or argument. Rome reads as if the same author wrote it.
  • Strata: 4.76, 4.65 and 4.73, no report with more than one factual error, so all three met the rule fixed on 27 October (mean at least 4.4, at most one factual error per report). The 44 unsampled reports became "Opus 5.5, reviewed with Fable 5.1 (sampled)".
  • Applied with the clearance: the review_notes corrections to 3.1 (Livy's sources "besides Fabius Pictor") and 6.1 (Judge's reading and the aureus of 28), and 4.1's treaty box now names Beaumont, as 1.6 does, not Walbank.

Rome 10.5, the capstone

  • Audited on its own: all 40 model answers read against their home reports (including the twelve corrected today), 35 claims checked directly, every pointer opened. 4.7, pass, no factual error; two gap-note pointers tidied (gap 7 now names Ardashir and module 7.4; gap 1 says 11.3 fills its part "once written"). The auditor's one structural note, that six Part A questions reuse core self-tests, is left for the maintainer. Off the queue: no Rome report is flagged.

Greek corrections applied (Fable-written, no flag involved)

  • 7.2's Epicurean gods box (Konstan realist; Long and Sedley idealist); 7.3's Veii statuettes "of disputed date" and the 88 BC massacre figures (80,000 in Valerius Maximus and Memnon, 150,000 in Plutarch); 9.3's Ovide moralisé early fourteenth-century; 9.4's exam allowance "about four and a quarter hours"; 6.3's 3,000 prisoners credited to Plutarch; 8.1's Q8 answer. 7.2 and 8.1 still fail check.py on length, as before.

Two rules from Pedro, afternoon of 27 October

  • Length is a guide, not a law. A topic takes the words it needs, 2,000 or 6,000. check.py now reports the count against the target as a note and never fails on it; AGENT-SPEC.md, WRITER-BRIEF.md and the rubric (dimension 8 no longer scores length; padding is the fault) say so. On the new rule Greek 2.1 is a 5 on style and the baseline mean 4.9; no verdict in this session changes.
  • Every unit keeps its sources and bibliography for future checks. New biblio.py, run by build.py, extracts from every report's inline citations a per-unit list (bibliography/<course>/unit-<n>.md, 26 files covering all 137 reports): ancient works with locators, authors cited by bare locator, modern works with title and year, corpora and inscriptions, per report. Machine-made, so a check aid. Every new report's writer now also leaves a curated bibliography/<course>/r-<id>.md (WRITER-BRIEF.md); the unit file links to it. Curated files for the 137 existing reports were then written the same day by Sonnet 5.5 agents, one per unit group, to bibliography/BIBLIO-BRIEF.md; they are flagged in provenance.json artefacts until Fable 5.1 spot-checks about three units.

Kit

  • New: review_due.py, biblio.py and bibliography/, audits/AUDIT-BRIEF.md, thirteen scorecards, audits/rome-14.4-cohorts.md. README.md, BACKLOG.md, AGENT-SPEC.md updated for the alert; NEXT-SESSION.md rewritten to resume at Greek 13.2.

25 October 2026Session 7The three checks and this changelog

The checks that session 6 could not finish, then this page.

The checks

  • Rome 10.5 (the capstone exam), 13.1 and 13.2 each had a fresh checker that had not seen it written. 10.5's checker used an exam version of the brief: coverage of units 0 to 10 and of the Greek course, every model answer tested against the reports it draws on, every pointer opened, and each of the ten gaps tested for whether it is real.
  • All three came back "fix": 9 errors, 10 should-fix and 21 minor findings. Each was verified before it was applied, and all were applied.
  • 13.1: Laelius, not Scipio, recalls the talks with Panaetius (On the Republic 1.34). The Posidonius box gave a false reason for the Brutus' silence. Carneades' two speeches are cited to Lactantius, not Plutarch. Eight minor fixes.
  • 13.2: in On Fate 10–11 Socrates does not answer Zopyrus; his reply is in the Tusculan Disputations (4.80). Four minor fixes.
  • 10.5, errors:
    • The opening box described positions no answer takes.
    • One answer said every writer counted the consular colleges.
    • Another had all of Livy's sources writing after 133, but he names Fabius Pictor.
    • The twins were dated to the first written records of about 200 BC, but Callias named them about 300.
    • One follow-up prompt duplicated units 12 and 13.
  • 10.5, other changes:
    • The timings add up to four and a quarter hours, not three.
    • Slavery, named in the prompt, now has a question.
    • Two follow-up prompts were replaced.
    • Generals other than Ricimer no longer make emperors.
  • All three were re-registered as "Opus 5.5". The site has no "unchecked" pills (there were 12), and the 20 queued modules keep their "next" pills.
  • The checker also found slips in reports outside this session's job. They are logged, not edited: Greek 9.4's exam timings (Fable 5.1, for the Fable review), and Rome 1.4, 3.1 and 6.1.
  • NEXT-SESSION.md now covers only the 20 modules still to write. The site was published as version 32.

The changelog

  • CHANGELOG.md was added to the kit, and the site gained a Changelog page built from it. It is linked from the home page and from the drawer on the home page. Each session is a card, and the newest is open.
  • shell.js now keeps the prev and next arrows hidden on the root pages, and verify.py checks the changelog page.
  • From now on each session sends only the kit zip; NEXT-SESSION.md and CHANGELOG.md travel inside it.
25 October 2026Session 6Rome units 8 to 10, Greek deep dive III, the first Rome deep dives

The biggest writing session so far: 28 reports. It stopped twice on usage limits, resumed both times, and was stopped a third time by an interruption.

Site and tooling

  • Two bugs found by the re-check at the end of session 5 were fixed. The Fable flag matched provenance keys badly in build.py, and verify.py assumed Greek 11.8 was always the last report.
  • AGENT-SPEC.md was made course-general, with the philosophy citation conventions for both courses: Diels–Kranz, Stephanus, Bekker, SVF, Long and Sedley, Diogenes Laertius, Sextus, Plotinus, and Seneca, Epictetus and Marcus.
  • WRITER-BRIEF.md asks every writer for a notes file listing its unsure claims, and a checker brief was written (reviews/units-9-13/BRIEF.md).
  • check.py gained a capstone mode, build.py names partial syllabus entries by their flag label, and unit cards read "in progress" while partly written.
  • New pipeline: every report has a writer, then a fresh checker that has not seen it written, then a maintainer who verifies each finding before applying it.

Rome

  • Unit 8, crisis and the late empire (8.1 to 8.6): 8.2 keep, the rest fix. 7 errors, 4 should-fix, 30 minor.
  • Unit 9, law, army and daily life (9.1 to 9.5): all fix. 8 errors, 12 should-fix, 26 minor.
  • 10.1 to 10.4, Ovid, imperial literature, Roman art and reception: all fix. 4 errors, 9 should-fix, 10 minor.
  • Deep dive I, 11.1 and 11.2 (the assemblies, the army in politics): 11.1 keep, 11.2 fix. 1 error, 4 should-fix and 9 minor between them.
  • 10.5 (the capstone exam), 13.1 and 13.2: written, but their checks were stopped by the interruption. They were registered as "unchecked", with an orange pill on the site. The 20 modules still to write got a blue "next" pill.

Greece

  • Deep dive III, Greek philosophy (12.1 to 12.8): the first Greek unit with numbered cross-references. 12.3 keep, the rest fix. 7 errors, 22 should-fix, 43 minor.
  • The Greek course was complete at 78 reports. Its syllabus was republished as version 7, with unit 13's line naming unit 12.
  • Two corrections to Fable-written Greek reports were found and logged for the Fable review, not applied: 7.2's Epicurean gods box has the scholars' names reversed, and 9.3 dates the Ovide moralisé a century too early.

Published

  • The site after each unit; version 29 carried Rome units 8 and 9, 10.1 to 10.4 and Greek 12.1 to 12.5. The last publish of the session carried the unchecked and next pills.
25 October 2026Session 5Philosophy in both syllabi, Rome unit 7, the quality pass

Syllabi

  • The changes planned in SYLLABUS-CHANGES.md were applied:
    • Rome gained deep dive III, Roman philosophy, the Stoics and Marcus Aurelius (unit 13).
    • Greece gained deep dive III, Greek philosophy (unit 12).
    • The Aeneid prompts were tightened.
    • The audits were renumbered: Rome unit 14, Greek unit 13.
  • Rome now has 15 units and 85 modules (79 content, 6 audit); Greece has 14 units and 83 modules (78 content, 5 audit).
  • Two facts in the plan were corrected first: Cato's Athenodorus and Augustus' Athenodorus were two men (Strabo 14.5.14), and "at Carnuntum" stands between books 2 and 3 of the Meditations.
  • A review of the reshaped syllabi found 4 errors, 29 should-fix and 15 minor problems; all were applied.

Rome unit 7, the Principate, AD 14 to 235

  • 7.1 to 7.5, about 15,500 words. Rome stood at 39 of 79 reports, about 115,000 words.

The quality pass

  • All 39 Rome reports were read by ten fresh reviewer agents, three to five reports each, grouped by unit. None had written or seen the reports.
  • Verdicts: 13 keep, 26 fix, none to rewrite. Findings: 41 errors, 62 should-fix and 106 minor. Each was checked before it was applied (reviews/2026-quality-pass/).
  • The recurring slips became rules in AGENT-SPEC.md:
    • a modern book's year or title not matching the edition named;
    • an ancient author cited for what only a later one says;
    • a Self-test answer contradicting a corrected body;
    • a dispute box with one side unnamed;
    • a figure without a source.
  • crosscheck.py greece found seven pairs of Greek reports with shared passages and six disputes told in several reports. They were logged for the Fable review.

Published, then planned

  • The site and both syllabi were published.
  • A re-check of the site found two bugs (fixed in session 6). NEXT-SESSION.md was rewritten for Rome units 8 to 13 and Greek unit 12, with a lane note for each module listing what the written reports already hand to it.
24 to 25 October 2026Session 4Rome units 5 and 6, the cohesion review

Reports

  • Unit 5, the fall of the Republic (5.1 to 5.6): the Gracchi; Marius and Sulla; Pompey, Crassus, Cicero and Catiline; Caesar; Antony, Octavian and Cleopatra; writing in a falling Republic.
  • Unit 6, Augustus (6.1 to 6.4): from Octavian to Augustus; the city of marble and the restored religion; the Augustan writers and their myth; empire without end.
  • Fixed as they landed:
    • 5.2 was cut from 5,233 to 3,184 words.
    • Marius' equestrian family was sourced to Velleius.
    • The Clodius hand-offs were repointed to deep dives 11.3 and 11.5.
    • A false pointer to 0.1 was removed.
    • Wiseman's attribution on Valerius Cato was softened.

Cohesion review

  • crosscheck.py was added: it lists every cross-reference with its context, finds shared passages and flags overlapping dispute boxes.
  • It was run over all Rome reports, 0.1 to 6.4:
    • Three pointers that misdescribed their targets were fixed.
    • Two duplications were given one home each (Ennius' augury; Tarquin's poppies).
    • The citation style was normalised across 13 reports.
    • "Augustus" for 29 BC was corrected.

Planned

  • SYLLABUS-CHANGES.md set out the philosophy deep dives for both syllabi, and NEXT-SESSION.md set out Rome unit 7 and a full quality pass.

22 to 24 October 2026Session 1The Greek course, then Personal Mythology

It began on 22 October with a request for a syllabus to learn Greek history and mythology in depth, with a prompt for each topic.

Hellas: the syllabus and the core

  • The syllabus, 22 October: ten units and 50 modules, each with a one-line reason and a drop-in prompt, published as its own page.
  • Units 0 to 2: twelve reports written in parallel and assembled into one tabbed page.
  • Units 3 to 6: 37 reports, 114,729 words.
  • Unit 7: 41 reports, 126,137 words.
  • Units 8 and 9: ending with the capstone exam, 9.4. This finished the core's 50 reports.

The site takes shape

  • Mobile redesign: a fixed top bar with prev and next, and a full-height slide-out drawer grouped by unit, its units closed by default.
  • Light mode with a sun and moon toggle.
  • 23 October: the site opens on Home, with a Continue button. A Syllabus page shows every prompt with a copy button and a link to its report.
  • The home page restructured:
    • a to-scale timeline from 3000 BC to AD 529;
    • four parts (ground rules, myths, history, practice and legacy) plus the deep dives;
    • collapsible unit cards with progress bars;
    • "soon" rows in the drawer.
    • A drawer bug that filed 10.1 to 10.6 under unit 1 was fixed.
  • A "Next steps" page listing the nine cultures planned after Greece.

The deep dives

  • Deep dive I, the Trojan War (unit 10, 12 modules) and deep dive II, Alexander (unit 11, 8 modules) were added to the syllabus, bringing it to 70 modules.
  • Troy was written in two halves: 62 of 70 reports, about 197,000 words.
  • 24 October: Alexander, 11.1 to 11.8. The course was complete at 70 reports, about 219,500 words. A writer published 11.6 as a stray page.

Personal Mythology

  • The project was renamed and reframed as Personal Mythology, a personal journey towards understanding mythos and logos.
  • The "Next steps" page became the root home page: a mythos and logos introduction, "How every course works", Greece as course 1 and nine cultures coming soon.
  • A home icon was added to the top bar. The drawer became context-aware: courses on the home page, one course's units inside it.
  • Unit 12, a quality audit (12.1 to 12.5), was added to the syllabus, bringing it to 75 modules.
  • provenance.json records which model wrote each report: Fable 5.1 wrote 55, and Opus 5.5 wrote 15 (10.4, 10.7 to 10.12, all of unit 11). Each report shows its model at the foot.
  • The spacing rule: at least 6 px between stacked boxes, checked by a script on all 73 pages.
  • The source was packaged as a kit that rebuilds the site byte for byte, with the first handoff prompt, for Rome units 0 and 1.

BACKLOG · the queue of chats · 17 chats done

Backlog

The ordered queue of chats for the Learning Project. One row is one chat. Take the top row that is not done; paste its prompt into a new chat in the Project on the model named; when the chat ends, move the row to Done with the date and commit. Specs are in pm-status-2026-10-28.md; the rule for keeping this file and CHANGELOG.md current is method.md §8: a finished row moves to the changelog, tasks uncovered are added as proposed rows, and the backlog is handed to Pedro at the end of every chat for confirmation of what is next. (kit/BACKLOG.md is a different file: the Rome writing prompt with the lane notes, used by S6 and its successors.)

Queue

Order decided 28 October 2026 (academy.md AD4, reworked after the Brand + AI hand-off landed in the repo; P4, S1 and A1 with P5 to P7, then P8 to P11 and A0, done the same day). Two lanes: the Academy lane (one chat at a time, in order) and the Brand + AI writing lane (one unit per chat, per docs/handoffs/2026-10-28-brand-ai-handoff.md step 2 and skills/). Academy›History›Course 1 · Greece
COURSE 1 · GREECE · Personal Mythology · COMPLETE

Greek history and mythology

Four parts and three deep dives, 78 modules, plus a quality audit. History and myth are interleaved on purpose: the myths only make sense once you know which society was telling them and why. Every module is a long-form report with named primary sources, the places where ancient accounts disagree, a ten-question self-test and three ideas to carry forward.

78of 78 reports written
13of 13 units complete
246kwords so far
18hours of reading
ContinueStart the course0.1 How we know anything: the sourcesSyllabusAll 83 prompts, copyable

Flagged for Fable 5.1 review: 6 of its 78 reports written with Opus 5.5. See unit 13.

3,500 years, to scale

3000 BC → AD 529
3000 BC1200 BCAD 1529

The Classical age that most people mean by "ancient Greece" is the narrow blue slice: about 150 years. The myths are set in the long orange band and were written down in the teal one.

  • Bronze Age3000 BC – 1200 BCunit 1, and the setting of the myths in units 2–4
  • Dark Age1200 BC – 800 BCmodule 1.3
  • Archaic800 BC – 480 BCunit 5
  • Classical480 BC – 323 BCunit 6
  • Hellenistic323 BC – 31 BCunit 7
  • Roman31 BC – AD 529modules 7.3 and 7.4

How to read it

five rules
  1. Parts I to IV in order. Each report assumes the ones before it. Part I is the foundation; skipping it is how the gaps got there. Part IV ends with a capstone exam across the whole course.
  2. Deep dives when you want them. Unit 10 (Troy) extends unit 4, unit 11 (Alexander) extends module 6.7, and unit 12 (Greek philosophy) extends modules 5.6, 6.5, 7.2 and 7.4. Take each straight after the material it extends, or after the capstone.
  3. One or two a sitting. A report is about 3,000 words, fifteen to twenty minutes. More than two a day and the self-tests stop meaning anything.
  4. Self-test before moving on. Under 7 of 10, re-read. The carry-forward ideas at the end of each report are the ones later reports lean on.
  5. A study text, not scripture. Citations are given so you can check them against the primary sources. Where sources disagree, the report says so in a red-edged box.
I
Units 0–16 modulesc. 3000 to 800 BC

Ground rules

Where the evidence comes from, how the land shaped everything, and the Bronze Age world that the myths half remember.

UNIT 0FoundationscompleteThree short modules that stop every later report from floating free of time, place and evidence.
0.1How we know anything: the sourcesYou need to know who is talking before you can weigh what they say.0.2Geography, dialects and the shape of GreeceThe polis, the colonies and half the myths are explained by mountains and sea.0.3The timeline spine, 3000 BC to AD 529Twelve dates you hang everything else on.
UNIT 1The Bronze Age and the long darkcompleteThe world the myths remember. Mycenae, Knossos and Troy were real places; the stories are what a later, poorer Greece made of the ruins.
1.1Minoan CreteThe first European palace civilisation, and the kernel of the Minotaur, Daedalus and Europa.1.2Mycenaean GreeceThe Greeks of the Iliad as they actually were: tablets, chariots and bureaucrats.1.3The collapse of 1200 and the Dark AgeFour centuries of forgetting. This gap is where history turned into myth.
II
Units 2–418 modulesset in the Bronze Age, told from c. 750 BC

The myths

Gods, heroes and the war at Troy, read as a later Greece told them, with each story's sources and the city that gained from it.

UNIT 2Mythology I: the cosmos and the godscompleteHesiod's Theogony is the backbone. Learn the genealogy properly once and every later story has a place to sit.
2.1Cosmogony: Chaos to ZeusThe succession myth, and its Near Eastern parents.2.2The Twelve OlympiansOne profile each, with cult and epithets, not just the comic-book version.2.3The underworld and the chthonic godsHades, Persephone, Demeter's grief, and the geography of the dead.2.4Prometheus, Pandora, the Five Ages and the FloodWhere humans come from and why life is hard. Hesiod's answer to the problem of evil.2.5The lesser divinities and personificationsThe crowded middle of the pantheon that most summaries skip.2.6The monsters and their genealogyThey are a family, and the hero stories are mostly about killing the cousins.
UNIT 3Mythology II: the heroescompleteThe generation before Troy. Each hero is also a city's claim to importance; keep asking who told this story and what they got from it.
3.1What a hero is, and PerseusThe category first, then the cleanest example of the pattern.3.2HeraclesThe biggest hero and the most political one. Needs its own module.3.3Theseus and AthensA hero built to order in the 6th and 5th centuries. Myth as state propaganda.3.4Jason, the Argonauts and MedeaThe crew list is a who's who of the generation before Troy.3.5The Theban cycle: Cadmus to AntigoneFive generations of a cursed house. The other great epic cycle after Troy.3.6The other heroes: Bellerophon, Meleager, Atalanta, Pelops, TantalusThe secondary heroes, plus the origins of the house of Atreus before Troy needs it.3.7Love, hubris and metamorphosisThe stories everyone half-knows, and the Roman filter most of them came through.
UNIT 4Mythology III: the Trojan cyclecompleteThe Iliad and Odyssey are two episodes of a much longer story. Learn the whole arc, then read the two surviving poems as the masterpieces they are.
4.1Causes of the war and the gathering at AulisEverything that happens before the Iliad's first line.4.2The IliadFifty-one days of the tenth year. Book by book, then what it means.4.3The fall of Troy: the lost Epic CyclePenthesilea, Memnon, Achilles' death, the arms, the horse, the sack. All from poems we have only in summary.4.4The Returns and the OdysseyWhat happened to everyone else, then Odysseus in full.4.5The house of Atreus after TroyAgamemnon's murder, Orestes, and how tragedy turned a blood-feud into a theory of justice.
III
Units 5–717 modules800 BC to AD 529

The history

From the first poleis to the closing of the Academy: Sparta and Athens, Persia, the long war, Alexander, the kingdoms and Rome.

UNIT 5The Archaic age, 800 to 480 BCcompleteWriting comes back, the polis is invented, and the Greeks spread from Spain to the Crimea. This is when the myths were written down and when Sparta and Athens became what they were.
5.1The eighth-century renaissanceThe alphabet, Homer written down, Olympia, Delphi, the first temples.5.2The polis and colonisationThe unit of Greek politics and the expansion that built the Greek world.5.3Hoplites, lawgivers and tyrantsHow the Greeks fought, and the men who seized the poleis in the 7th and 6th centuries.5.4SpartaThe system, the myth of the system, and how to tell them apart.5.5Athens from Solon to CleisthenesThe hundred years that produced democracy, step by step.5.6Lyric poets and the first philosophersThe first "I" in Western literature, and the first people to explain the world without gods.
UNIT 6The Classical age, 480 to 323 BCcompleteThe century and a half everybody means by "ancient Greece". Persia, the Athenian empire, the long war, the theatre, the philosophers, then Macedon.
6.1The Persian WarsHerodotus' subject. Marathon, Thermopylae, Salamis, Plataea, and what they did to Greek self-image.6.2The Athenian empire and the radical democracyFifty years of Delian League, Pericles and the machinery of government by lot.6.3The Peloponnesian WarThucydides' war, 431 to 404. The plague, Sicily, the Thirty.6.4Tragedy and comedyThe theatre as civic institution, and the 33 surviving plays as a reading list.6.5The Sophists, Socrates, Plato and AristotleThe thinking that happened in the same city, in the same decades, as the war and the plays.6.6The fourth century: Sparta, Thebes, PhilipThe messy decades between Athens' fall and Alexander's rise, which most courses skip.6.7AlexanderThirteen years that ended the Classical world and started the Hellenistic one.
UNIT 7Hellenistic and Roman Greece, 323 BC to AD 529completeGreek becomes a world language, Alexandria replaces Athens, and then Rome arrives. The period that shaped how all later Greek learning reached us.
7.1The Successors and the kingdomsForty years of war over the corpse, then three dynasties.7.2Alexandria: science, scholarship and the new philosophiesThe Library, Euclid, Archimedes, and the Stoics and Epicureans who taught people how to live under kings.7.3Rome conquers GreeceFrom Pyrrhus to the sack of Corinth in 146, and the cultural counter-conquest.7.4Greece under the Empire and the end of antiquityPlutarch, Pausanias, Hadrian's Athens, the rise of Christianity, the closing of the temples.
IV
Units 8–99 modulesacross the whole span

Practice and legacy

What Greeks did at altars and in houses, what they built and painted, and how all of it reached you. Ends with the capstone exam.

UNIT 8Religion and daily lifecompleteMyth was not religion. Religion was what people did: sacrifice, festivals, oracles, mysteries. This unit is the practice behind the stories.
8.1Ritual practice: sacrifice, temples, priests, pollutionWhat a Greek actually did at an altar, and why there was no church, no creed, no scripture.8.2Oracles and mystery cultsDelphi, Dodona, Eleusis, Orphism, Dionysus. Where ordinary religion stopped and personal salvation began.8.3Festivals and the gamesThe Panathenaia, the four crown games, and sport as religion.8.4Death, the afterlife and hero cultWhat Greeks believed happened to them, and the cult of the dead that fed the myths.8.5Households, women, slaves, the symposium and sexualityThe social facts the myths assume. Athens mostly, because that is where the evidence is.
UNIT 9Art, the ground, and the afterlife of it allcompleteHow to read a temple and a vase, how to read a site, and how the whole thing reached you.
9.1Reading Greek art: temples, sculpture, vasesThe material record is the second great source for myth. Learn its grammar.9.2Reading a site: Athens on footThe Acropolis, the Agora, the Kerameikos and the National Museum as a walkable exam.9.3Reception: how it all reached youByzantium, the Renaissance, Winckelmann, Freud, and the modern retellings that put the gaps in your knowledge in the first place.9.4Capstone: the whole thing in one sittingThe final exam. Run it only after everything above.

Units 10–1228 modulesoptional

Deep dives

The Trojan War, Alexander and Greek philosophy taken to the depth the survey could not reach. Best after the units they extend, or after the capstone.

UNIT 10Deep dive I: the Trojan WarcompleteOptional extension after the core course, or after unit 4 if Troy is what you came for. Twelve modules that take the people, the poems, the place and the afterlife of the war to the depth the survey could not.
10.1AchillesThe best of the Achaeans, across every source that touched him.10.2Agamemnon and the kingship of the AtreidsWhat kind of king he is, why the army obeys him, and what the Bronze Age says about it.10.3Hector, Priam and the Trojan sideTroy from inside the walls: the royal house, the allies, the women, and whether the Trojans are foreign.10.4OdysseusFrom the Iliad's counsellor to the Odyssey's survivor to tragedy's liar, and on to Ulysses.10.5Helen, Menelaus and SpartaThe cause of the war, the woman who may never have gone, and the Spartan kingdom she came from.10.6Reading the Iliad closely: Books 1, 6, 9, 16, 18, 22, 24The seven books that carry the poem, scene by scene, with the lines worth knowing.10.7Reading the Odyssey closely: the Telemachy, the Apologoi, the Nekyia, the recognitionsThe poem's architecture and the scenes that hold it, with the lines worth knowing.10.8The other Greeks at TroyDiomedes, the two Ajaxes, Nestor, Patroclus, Antilochus, Idomeneus, Philoctetes, Neoptolemus, Teucer and the rest.10.9The gods at TroyZeus' plan, the partisans, Thetis, the theomachy, and whether the gods are to blame.10.10Troy the place: Hisarlik and the historicity questionNine cities on one mound, the Hittite archive, and how much of the war is history.10.11Homeric society and Homeric warfareWhose world is the Iliad? Battle mechanics, gift exchange, honour, households, slaves, women.10.12Troy after antiquityHow the Trojan War became Rome's founding myth, Europe's ancestry, and modern fiction.
UNIT 11Deep dive II: AlexandercompleteOptional extension after unit 6.7. Eight modules on the kingdom, the army, the enemy, the gods, the court, the east, the sources and the long afterlife.
11.1Macedon before AlexanderThe Argead kingdom, the Greek-or-not question, the court, and Philip's state in depth.11.2Alexander's generalship: the battles and siegesGranicus, Issus, Tyre, Gaugamela, the Hydaspes, tactically and logistically.11.3The Achaemenid sideDarius III, the empire he inherited, how Persia fought, and why it lost.11.4Alexander and the godsSiwa, Zeus-Ammon, proskynesis, divine honours, and the heroes he set out to surpass.11.5The court: Companions, conspiracies and the orientalising policyParmenion, Philotas, Cleitus, Callisthenes, the pages, Hephaestion, the Susa weddings, Opis.11.6The eastern campaign: Bactria, Sogdiana, India and the returnThe hardest years: guerrilla war, Roxane, the Hydaspes, the mutiny, the Mallian wound, Gedrosia, Nearchus.11.7The sources for AlexanderFive late authors resting on lost contemporaries: how we know what we think we know.11.8Death, body and afterlivesBabylon 323, the tomb, the Hellenistic Alexander, the Roman imitators, Iskandar, and the historians' Alexander.
UNIT 12Deep dive III: Greek philosophycompleteOptional extension after modules 5.6, 6.5, 7.2 and 7.4, or after the capstone. From Thales to Justinian's ban of 529: the arguments, the schools and the lives, at the depth the survey modules could only outline.
12.1The PresocraticsFrom Thales to Democritus argument by argument, and how a few hundred fragments became a canon.12.2The Sophists and SocratesThe professors of argument and the man who claimed no wisdom, read in what survives of them.12.3PlatoForms, souls, cities and myths: the dialogues argument by argument.12.4AristotleTwenty years with Plato, a royal pupil, and lecture notes that organised knowledge for two thousand years.12.5Cynics, Cyrenaics and the other Socratic schoolsDogs, hedonists and logicians: what Socrates' followers made of him.12.6Epicurus and the GardenAtoms, pleasure and the fear of death: a philosophy built to be memorised.12.7The Stoa from Zeno to PosidoniusFrom Zeno's painted colonnade to the teachers of Roman senators.12.8Sceptics and Neoplatonists: the late schools to 529The doubters and the mystics, from Pyrrho to the last head of the Academy.

The reading spine

primary and secondary

The reports keep naming these. Read the primary ones at least once each, in this order, any decent translation: Hesiod (Theogony, Works and Days) · Homer (Iliad, then Odyssey) · Apollodorus, The Library (Robin Hard's Oxford translation; keep it open through Part II) · Aeschylus' Oresteia, Sophocles' Theban plays, Euripides' Medea and Bacchae · Herodotus · Thucydides · Plato (Apology, Symposium, Republic; with unit 6 and deep dive 12) · Diogenes Laertius, books 6, 7 and 10 (with deep dive 12) · Plutarch's Lives as each name comes up · Pausanias, book 1.

Secondary, three only: Robin Hard, The Routledge Handbook of Greek Mythology; Walter Burkert, Greek Religion; Robin Osborne, Greece in the Making, with Simon Hornblower, The Greek World 479 to 323.

AcademyGreece is the first course. Rome, the second, is in progress; eight more are coming: Egypt, Persia, Norse, Celtic, English, Scottish, Chinese, Japanese.
Academy›Greece›Syllabus
GREECE · SYLLABUS · 83 modules · every prompt in full

Greece: syllabus and prompts

This is the complete syllabus the reports were generated from. Each module has its drop-in prompt; copy it into a new chat to regenerate a report yourself, extend one, or compare a fresh answer against the one here. Where a report exists it is linked.

Flagged for Fable 5.1 review: 6 of its 78 reports written with Opus 5.5. See unit 13.

How to run it

  1. Do the modules in order. Each prompt assumes the ones before it. Skipping ahead is how the gaps got there in the first place.
  2. Tap copy prompt, paste the prompt into a new chat here, read the report, do the self-test at the end. If you score under 7/10, re-read before moving on.
  3. Tick the box when done. Progress is stored on this phone only.
  4. Every prompt asks for named primary sources and for the places where ancient accounts contradict each other. There is no single canonical version of any Greek myth; knowing the variants is what "knowing the myth" means.

Units 10, 11 and 12 are deep dives: optional, and best taken after the core material they extend (unit 4; module 6.7; modules 5.6, 6.5, 7.2 and 7.4) or after the capstone.

Honest scoping: no syllabus gets you "all" of it. The corpus is thousands of local variants, half of them surviving as one line in a scholiast. What this gets you is the complete skeleton, the main sources read in the right order, and enough structure that anything you meet later snaps into place.

0 · Foundations1 · The Bronze Age and the long dark2 · Mythology I: the cosmos and the gods3 · Mythology II: the heroes4 · Mythology III: the Trojan cycle5 · The Archaic age, 800 to 480 BC6 · The Classical age, 480 to 323 BC7 · Hellenistic and Roman Greece, 323 BC to AD 5298 · Religion and daily life9 · Art, the ground, and the afterlife of it all10 · Deep dive I: the Trojan War11 · Deep dive II: Alexander12 · Deep dive III: Greek philosophy13 · Quality audit
UNIT 0

Foundations

Three short modules that stop every later report from floating free of time, place and evidence.

0.1

How we know anything: the sources

You need to know who is talking before you can weigh what they say.

Give me an in-depth report on the sources for ancient Greek mythology and history. Cover: the literary sources in order (Homer, Hesiod, the Homeric Hymns, the lost Epic Cycle, the lyric poets, the tragedians, Herodotus, Thucydides, Apollodorus' Library, Pausanias, Plutarch, the scholia and mythographers, and Roman transmitters like Ovid and Hyginus); material sources (vase painting, sculpture, inscriptions, Linear B, archaeology); what "myth" meant to Greeks and why there was never one canonical version; how a modern scholar decides between variants; and the main traps (treating Ovid as Greek, treating Apollodorus as early, treating Homer as history). Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, name specific works and dates, flag disagreements between sources, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
0.2

Geography, dialects and the shape of Greece

The polis, the colonies and half the myths are explained by mountains and sea.

Give me an in-depth report on the geography of the ancient Greek world and how it shaped Greek history and myth. Cover: the regions of mainland Greece (Attica, Boeotia, Thessaly, the Peloponnese and its parts, Epirus, Macedon), the islands (Cyclades, Crete, Euboea, Lesbos, Rhodes), Ionia and the Anatolian coast, Magna Graecia and Sicily, the Black Sea; the three dialect families (Ionic-Attic, Doric, Aeolic) and what the "Dorian invasion" story claims versus the evidence; why mountains and sea produced small independent poleis; which myths are tied to which places (Delphi, Mycenae, Thebes, Crete, Troy, Ithaca, Olympia, Eleusis) and why. Include a text description of a mental map I can memorise. Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
0.3

The timeline spine, 3000 BC to AD 529

Twelve dates you hang everything else on.

Give me an in-depth report that builds a memorisable timeline of ancient Greek history from the Early Bronze Age to the closing of the Academy in AD 529. Cover: the standard period names and their date ranges (Minoan, Mycenaean, Bronze Age collapse, Dark Age / Early Iron Age, Archaic, Classical, Hellenistic, Roman Greece, Late Antique); the 12 to 15 anchor dates every educated person should know, each with one sentence on why it matters; how the ancient Greeks themselves dated things (Olympiads, archon years, Trojan War dating by Eratosthenes); the big debates about the chronology (the Thera eruption date, the collapse of 1200, when Homer was written down). Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, a clean dated list I can copy out by hand, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
UNIT 1

The Bronze Age and the long dark

The world the myths remember. Mycenae, Knossos and Troy were real places; the stories are what a later, poorer Greece made of the ruins.

1.1

Minoan Crete

The first European palace civilisation, and the kernel of the Minotaur, Daedalus and Europa.

Give me an in-depth report on Minoan Crete, c. 3000 to 1450 BC. Cover: the palaces (Knossos, Phaistos, Malia, Zakros) and what a "palace" actually was; Linear A and why it is unread; religion as reconstructed from frescoes, seals and peak sanctuaries (the bull, the double axe, the snake goddess, the labyrinth); the Thera eruption and its dating problem; the Mycenaean takeover of Crete; Arthur Evans and how much of "Minoan" is his invention; and the myths later Greeks attached to Crete (Minos, Europa, Pasiphae, Daedalus and Icarus, Theseus and the Minotaur, Talos, the birth of Zeus on Ida) with a serious discussion of what, if anything, they preserve. Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
1.2

Mycenaean Greece

The Greeks of the Iliad as they actually were: tablets, chariots and bureaucrats.

Give me an in-depth report on Mycenaean Greece, c. 1600 to 1200 BC. Cover: the shaft graves and Schliemann at Mycenae; the palace centres (Mycenae, Tiryns, Pylos, Thebes, Knossos) and the citadel architecture; Linear B, its decipherment by Ventris and what the tablets reveal (the wanax, the lawagetas, slaves, textiles, chariots, and which Olympian gods already appear by name); Mycenaean religion and burial; the Hittite texts about Ahhiyawa and Wilusa and what they imply about a historical Troy; trade with Egypt and the Levant; and a sober assessment of how much Homer remembers of this world (boar's-tusk helmets, Nestor's cup) versus how much he invents. Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
1.3

The collapse of 1200 and the Dark Age

Four centuries of forgetting. This gap is where history turned into myth.

Give me an in-depth report on the Late Bronze Age collapse and the Greek Dark Age, c. 1200 to 800 BC. Cover: what collapsed and when (palace destructions, loss of writing, population crash) across the whole eastern Mediterranean; the competing explanations (Sea Peoples, drought, earthquakes, systems collapse, internal revolt) and the current scholarly weighting; what archaeology shows for the Dark Age (Lefkandi, Nichoria, the Protogeometric style); the Ionian migration and the "return of the Heracleidae"; and the central idea for this course: how an oral poetic tradition carried a distorted memory of the Mycenaean world across this gap, so that the heroic age became a lost golden age. Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
UNIT 2

Mythology I: the cosmos and the gods

Hesiod's Theogony is the backbone. Learn the genealogy properly once and every later story has a place to sit.

2.1

Cosmogony: Chaos to Zeus

The succession myth, and its Near Eastern parents.

Give me an in-depth report on the Greek creation myth and the succession of divine generations, following Hesiod's Theogony as the spine. Cover: Chaos, Gaia, Tartarus, Eros, Erebus and Nyx; Ouranos and the castration; the Titans by name and function; Kronos and the swallowed children; the birth and hiding of Zeus; the Titanomachy, the Hundred-Handers and Cyclopes; Typhon; the Gigantomachy and why it is a different event; how Zeus distributes honours. Then compare systematically with the Hittite Kumarbi cycle, the Babylonian Enuma Elish and Genesis, and explain what the parallels tell us about where Greek myth came from. Note where the Orphic cosmogony and Homer's version (Oceanus as origin) diverge from Hesiod. Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, a family tree in text form, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
2.2

The Twelve Olympians

One profile each, with cult and epithets, not just the comic-book version.

Give me an in-depth report on the twelve Olympian gods, one structured profile each: Zeus, Hera, Poseidon, Demeter, Athena, Apollo, Artemis, Ares, Aphrodite, Hephaestus, Hermes, and Dionysus (and explain the Hestia/Dionysus substitution and why "twelve" is a convention). For each: birth myth, domains, the most important epithets and what they reveal (Zeus Xenios, Athena Polias, Apollo Pythios, etc.), main cult centres and festivals, attributes in art, the two or three defining myths, and the places where Homer, Hesiod and the Homeric Hymns disagree. Finish with a section on what an Olympian is as a category: what makes these gods different from the Titans, the chthonic gods and the heroes. Format: about 3000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
2.3

The underworld and the chthonic gods

Hades, Persephone, Demeter's grief, and the geography of the dead.

Give me an in-depth report on the Greek underworld and the chthonic divinities. Cover: Hades the god and Hades the place; the abduction of Persephone and the Homeric Hymn to Demeter in detail, including its link to Eleusis and the seasons; the geography of the underworld (Styx, Acheron, Lethe, Cocytus, Phlegethon, Charon, Cerberus, the judges Minos, Rhadamanthys and Aeacus, Tartarus, the Asphodel Meadows, Elysium and the Isles of the Blessed) and how this geography changed from Homer to Virgil; the famous punished (Tantalus, Sisyphus, Ixion, Tityos, the Danaids); the katabasis tradition (Odysseus, Heracles, Orpheus, Theseus and Pirithous); Hecate, the Erinyes, Thanatos and Hypnos; and the Olympian/chthonic distinction in ritual (raised altar and white victim versus low pit and black victim). Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
2.4

Prometheus, Pandora, the Five Ages and the Flood

Where humans come from and why life is hard. Hesiod's answer to the problem of evil.

Give me an in-depth report on the Greek myths of human origins and the human condition. Cover: the Prometheus cycle in full (the trick at Mekone, the theft of fire, the eagle, the release by Heracles, and Aeschylus' Prometheus Bound as a reinterpretation); Pandora in Hesiod's Theogony versus Works and Days and the problem of the jar; the Myth of the Five Ages (gold, silver, bronze, heroes, iron) and its Near Eastern and Indo-European parallels; Deucalion and Pyrrha and the flood; the origin of named peoples from Hellen's sons (Dorus, Aeolus, Xuthus, Ion, Achaeus) and what this ethnic genealogy was for; and Plato's Prometheus myth in the Protagoras as a philosophical rewrite. Discuss Hesiod's pessimism and his idea of justice (dike) as a worldview. Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

The crowded middle of the pantheon that most summaries skip.

Give me an in-depth report on the lesser Greek divinities, organised by type. 2.6

The monsters and their genealogy

They are a family, and the hero stories are mostly about killing the cousins.

Give me an in-depth report on the monsters of Greek mythology as a genealogical system. Cover: Phorcys and Ceto and their children (the Graiae, the Gorgons, Echidna, Ladon); Typhon and Echidna's brood (Orthrus, Cerberus, the Hydra, the Chimaera, the Sphinx, the Nemean Lion, the Crommyonian Sow); the Cyclopes in Hesiod versus Homer; the Giants; the Centaurs and their origin from Ixion; the Harpies, Sirens, Scylla and Charybdis; the Minotaur; Medusa's children Pegasus and Chrysaor; Argus Panoptes; Talos; and the sea monsters Cetus. Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, a family tree in text form, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

UNIT 3

Mythology II: the heroes

The generation before Troy. Each hero is also a city's claim to importance; keep asking who told this story and what they got from it.

3.1

What a hero is, and Perseus

The category first, then the cleanest example of the pattern.

Give me an in-depth report in two parts. Part one: what a hero was to the Greeks: a semi-divine mortal, usually with a divine parent, honoured with cult at a tomb after death; the standard hero pattern (unusual birth, exposure, foreign upbringing, quest, monster, return, violent or strange death, cult); and how this differs from the modern meaning. Part two: Perseus in full, as the cleanest example of the pattern: Acrisius and the oracle, Danae and the golden rain, the chest, Polydectes, the Graiae and the gifts, Medusa, Andromeda and Cetus, the return and the discus, and the founding of Mycenae and the Perseid dynasty that leads to Heracles. Name the sources (Pherecydes, Apollodorus, Pindar, Ovid) and where they differ. Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
3.2

Heracles

The biggest hero and the most political one. Needs its own module.

Give me an in-depth report on Heracles, the complete life. Cover: birth (Alcmene, Amphitryon, Hera's hatred, the serpents), youth, the madness and the killing of his children; the Twelve Labours in the canonical order with the location, the monster or task, and what each labour is "about"; the parerga (side adventures: the Argonauts, Prometheus, Antaeus, Busiris, the Pillars); the servitude to Omphale, the sack of Troy and Oechalia; Deianira, Nessus and the poisoned robe; the pyre on Oeta and the apotheosis; his marriage to Hebe. Then the politics: why Dorian Sparta and the Macedonian kings claimed descent from him, the Heracleidae, and why Athens kept him too. Sources: Apollodorus, Diodorus, Euripides' Heracles, Sophocles' Women of Trachis, Pindar. Format: about 3000 words, headed sections, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

3.3

Theseus and Athens

A hero built to order in the 6th and 5th centuries. Myth as state propaganda.

Give me an in-depth report on Theseus. Cover: birth at Troezen, the sword and sandals, the six labours on the road to Athens, Medea at Aegeus' court, the Marathonian bull, the Cretan tribute, Ariadne, the Labyrinth and the Minotaur, Naxos, the black sail and Aegeus' death; the later deeds (the Amazons and Hippolyta, the Centauromachy at Pirithous' wedding, the abduction of Helen, the descent to Hades, Phaedra and Hippolytus, death on Scyros); and the "synoikismos" and his invention as the founder of Athenian democracy. Then the crucial part: how and why Athens manufactured Theseus as a rival to Dorian Heracles in the 6th to 5th centuries (Cimon bringing the bones back from Scyros in 476, the Theseion, the vase evidence, Bacchylides, Plutarch's Life). Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

Five generations of a cursed house. The other great epic cycle after Troy.

UNIT 4

Mythology III: the Trojan cycle

The Iliad and Odyssey are two episodes of a much longer story. Learn the whole arc, then read the two surviving poems as the masterpieces they are.

4.1

Causes of the war and the gathering at Aulis

Everything that happens before the Iliad's first line.

Give me an in-depth report on the origins of the Trojan War up to the Greek landing. Cover: Troy's founding and the Trojan royal line (Dardanus, Tros, Ilus, Laomedon, Priam and his children); the wedding of Peleus and Thetis and the apple of Eris; the Judgement of Paris; Helen's birth, the suitors and the oath of Tyndareus; the abduction (and the Egyptian Helen of Stesichorus and Euripides); the recruitment (Odysseus' feigned madness, Achilles on Scyros); Telephus; Iphigenia at Aulis (the sacrifice versus the substitution at the altar); Philoctetes abandoned on Lemnos; Protesilaus; and the first nine years. Set out the lost poems of the Epic Cycle (Cypria especially) and what we know of them from Proclus' summaries. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
4.2

The Iliad

Fifty-one days of the tenth year. Book by book, then what it means.

Give me an in-depth report on Homer's Iliad. Cover: a book-by-book summary of all 24 books in two or three sentences each; the structure (the ring composition, the three days of battle, the Embassy, the death of Patroclus as hinge, the Shield, the duel, the ransom); the main characters and what each represents (Achilles, Agamemnon, Hector, Priam, Helen, Andromache, Diomedes, Ajax, Odysseus, Nestor, Patroclus, Paris); the gods' role and the question of fate versus Zeus; the themes (menis, kleos, time, pity, mortality, the heroic code and its breakdown); the formulaic oral style and the Parry-Lord theory; the Homeric Question and the current view on dating and composition; and what the poem deliberately leaves out (no horse, no fall of Troy, no death of Achilles). Format: about 3000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

4.3

Penthesilea, Memnon, Achilles' death, the arms, the horse, the sack. All from poems we have only in summary.

Give me an in-depth report on the end of the Trojan War as told in the lost poems of the Epic Cycle (Aethiopis, Little Iliad, Iliou Persis) and their later retellings. Cover: Penthesilea and the Amazons; Memnon and the Ethiopians and the weighing of souls; the death of Achilles (Paris' arrow, the heel in late sources only) and his funeral; the Judgement of the Arms and Ajax's madness and suicide (Sophocles' Ajax); the recall of Philoctetes and the death of Paris; Neoptolemus brought from Scyros; the theft of the Palladium; the Wooden Horse, Laocoön, Sinon and Cassandra; the sack: the deaths of Priam and Astyanax, the rape of Cassandra, the sacrifice of Polyxena, the division of the captive women (Euripides' Trojan Women and Hecuba); the escape of Aeneas as later Roman claim. Agamemnon's murder, Orestes, and how tragedy turned a blood-feud into a theory of justice.

Give me an in-depth report on the aftermath of Troy in the house of Atreus. Cover: the murder of Agamemnon and Cassandra by Clytemnestra and Aegisthus (and the older Homeric version where Aegisthus is the main killer); Electra and the exile of Orestes; the matricide; the pursuit by the Erinyes; the trial at Athens and the founding of the Areopagus; Iphigenia among the Taurians; Orestes and Hermione, the death of Neoptolemus at Delphi. UNIT 5

The Archaic age, 800 to 480 BC

Writing comes back, the polis is invented, and the Greeks spread from Spain to the Crimea. This is when the myths were written down and when Sparta and Athens became what they were.

5.1

The eighth-century renaissance

The alphabet, Homer written down, Olympia, Delphi, the first temples.

Give me an in-depth report on the Greek "renaissance" of the eighth century BC. Cover: the adoption of the Phoenician alphabet and its earliest evidence (Nestor's cup, the Dipylon jug); the Euboeans, Al Mina and Pithekoussai; the fixing of the Homeric poems in writing and the Peisistratid recension question; Hesiod as a datable person and the world of Works and Days; the rise of the Panhellenic sanctuaries (Olympia and the 776 date, Delphi, Delos, Isthmia, Nemea) and why shared festivals mattered to a people with no shared state; the first stone temples and the Geometric style; hero cult at Mycenaean tombs beginning in this century and what it means for myth; and the population surge. Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
5.2

The polis and colonisation

The unit of Greek politics and the expansion that built the Greek world.

Give me an in-depth report on the rise of the polis and the Greek colonisation movement, c. 750 to 550 BC. Cover: what a polis was (citizen body, territory, agora, acropolis, laws, cults) and how it differed from a city; Aristotle's definition; the role of the oikos, phratry, tribe and deme; citizenship, metics and slaves in outline; the mechanics of founding a colony (oikist, Delphi's role, mother-city ties); the main waves and sites (Sicily and Italy: Cumae, Syracuse, Taras, Sybaris, Croton; the Black Sea: Byzantium, Sinope, Olbia; Cyrene; Massalia; Naucratis in Egypt); why they went (land, trade, stasis); and how colonisation shaped a "Greek" identity, the spread of myths to new places, and relations with Phoenicians and Etruscans. Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
5.3

Hoplites, lawgivers and tyrants

How the Greeks fought, and the men who seized the poleis in the 7th and 6th centuries.

Give me an in-depth report on Archaic Greek warfare and politics. Cover: the hoplite panoply and the phalanx, the debate over whether the "hoplite revolution" caused political change, and the Lelantine War; the lawgivers (Zaleucus, Draco, Lycurgus as legend) and the first written law codes (Gortyn); the age of tyrants, with Cypselus and Periander of Corinth, Pheidon of Argos, Polycrates of Samos, Cleisthenes of Sicyon, Pittacus of Mytilene and the Peisistratids of Athens; what "tyrannos" meant then versus later; why tyranny arose and how it ended; the Seven Sages; and the emergence of coinage from Lydia. Draw on Herodotus, Aristotle's Politics and Athenian Constitution, and Thucydides' Archaeology. Format: about 2000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

The hundred years that produced democracy, step by step.
