UNIT 6

The Classical age, 480 to 323 BC

The century and a half everybody means by "ancient Greece". Persia, the Athenian empire, the long war, the theatre, the philosophers, then Macedon.

6.1

The Persian Wars

Herodotus' subject. Marathon, Thermopylae, Salamis, Plataea, and what they did to Greek self-image.

Give me an in-depth report on the Persian Wars, 499 to 479 BC, following Herodotus. Cover: the Persian Empire under Cyrus, Cambyses and Darius in outline; the Ionian Revolt and the burning of Sardis; the first expedition and Marathon in detail (Miltiades, the run, the Spartans late); the interwar decade: Themistocles, the silver of Laurion and the fleet, ostracism in practice; Xerxes' invasion: the canal, the bridges, Thermopylae and Leonidas, Artemisium, the evacuation of Athens, Salamis, Plataea and Mycale; the Serpent Column; Herodotus himself: his method, his sources, his biases, his idea of why the Greeks won, and how far to trust him; and the war's effect on Greek identity (Greek versus barbarian, freedom versus slavery) and on the Athenian claim to leadership. Fifty years of Delian League, Pericles and the machinery of government by lot.

6.5

The Sophists, Socrates, Plato and Aristotle

The thinking that happened in the same city, in the same decades, as the war and the plays.

Give me an in-depth report on Athenian philosophy from the Sophists to Aristotle. 6.6

The fourth century: Sparta, Thebes, Philip

The messy decades between Athens' fall and Alexander's rise, which most courses skip.

Give me an in-depth report on Greece from 404 to 336 BC. Use Xenophon's Hellenica, Diodorus, Demosthenes and Plutarch. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

Alexander

Thirteen years that ended the Classical world and started the Hellenistic one.

UNIT 7

Hellenistic and Roman Greece, 323 BC to AD 529

Greek becomes a world language, Alexandria replaces Athens, and then Rome arrives. The period that shaped how all later Greek learning reached us.

7.1

The Successors and the kingdoms

Forty years of war over the corpse, then three dynasties.

Give me an in-depth report on the Wars of the Diadochi and the Hellenistic kingdoms. Cover: the settlement at Babylon, Perdiccas, Antipater, Craterus, Eumenes, Antigonus the One-Eyed and Demetrius the Besieger, Seleucus, Ptolemy, Lysimachus, Cassander and the murder of Alexander's family; Ipsus in 301 and Corupedium in 281; the three kingdoms and how each ruled: Ptolemaic Egypt (Alexandria, the pharaoh-king, the Greek elite), the Seleucid empire (the cities, the eastern losses, Antiochus III and IV, the Maccabees), Antigonid Macedon; the smaller powers (Pergamon and the Attalids, Rhodes, the Aetolian and Achaean Leagues, Sparta's revolution under Agis and Cleomenes); ruler cult and the new idea of kingship; and the Greco-Bactrian and Indo-Greek kingdoms as the far edge. Use Diodorus, Plutarch's Demetrius and Pyrrhus, Polybius. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
7.2

Alexandria: science, scholarship and the new philosophies

The Library, Euclid, Archimedes, and the Stoics and Epicureans who taught people how to live under kings.

Give me an in-depth report on Hellenistic culture and thought. Cover: the Museum and Library of Alexandria, the scholars who edited Homer (Zenodotus, Aristophanes of Byzantium, Aristarchus) and how that produced the text we read; Hellenistic poetry (Callimachus, Theocritus, Apollonius) and the shift to learned, small-scale, allusive verse that retold myths for a literate audience; science: Euclid, Archimedes, Eratosthenes measuring the earth, Aristarchus' heliocentrism, Hipparchus, Herophilus and dissection, Hero, the Antikythera mechanism; the philosophical schools: Stoicism (Zeno, Chrysippus; logos, virtue, the passions, cosmopolitanism), Epicureanism (atoms, the gods, pleasure as absence of pain, the fear of death), Scepticism (Pyrrho, the Academy under Arcesilaus and Carneades), and the Cynics; and the koine Greek and Septuagint as the medium of the Hellenistic world. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

7.3

Rome conquers Greece

From Pyrrhus to the sack of Corinth in 146, and the cultural counter-conquest.

Give me an in-depth report on the Roman conquest of the Greek world, 280 to 30 BC. Cover: Pyrrhus in Italy; the Illyrian wars; the Macedonian Wars (Cynoscephalae in 197, Flamininus' "freedom of the Greeks" at the Isthmian Games, Pydna in 168 and the end of the Antigonids, the deportation of the thousand Achaeans including Polybius); the Syrian War and Magnesia; the Attalid bequest of Pergamon; the sack of Corinth in 146 and the province of Macedonia; Mithridates and the sack of Athens by Sulla in 86; Pompey and the Seleucid end; Cleopatra, Actium and the end of Ptolemaic Egypt. Then the reverse conquest: Horace's "captive Greece captured her savage conqueror", the Greek tutors, the looting of art, the Roman adoption of Greek gods and myths (Jupiter for Zeus, Aeneas as Trojan ancestor), and Polybius' explanation of why Rome won. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

UNIT 8

Religion and daily life

Myth was not religion. Religion was what people did: sacrifice, festivals, oracles, mysteries. This unit is the practice behind the stories.

8.1

Ritual practice: sacrifice, temples, priests, pollution

What a Greek actually did at an altar, and why there was no church, no creed, no scripture.

Give me an in-depth report on the practice of ancient Greek religion. Cover: why there was no word for religion, no scripture, no creed, no priestly class with doctrine; the structure of animal sacrifice step by step (procession, lustral water, barley, the prayer, the cut, the division of the meat, the gods' portion) and the Prometheus myth as its explanation; libations, votives and first-fruits; the temple as the god's house, not a congregation hall, and the altar outside; priests and priestesses as civic offices; the household cults (Zeus Herkeios, Hestia, Hermes at the door); purity and pollution (miasma) from birth, death, murder and sex, and purification rites; curses, oaths, binding tablets and magic; prayer gestures; the calendar of an Athenian year; and how the state, the deme, the household and the individual each related to the gods. Draw on Burkert and Parker where relevant. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report

Delphi, Dodona, Eleusis, Orphism, Dionysus. Where ordinary religion stopped and personal salvation began.

Give me an in-depth report on Greek oracles and mystery cults. Cover: Delphi in depth (the Pythia, the procedure, the famous responses in Herodotus, the Amphictyony, the Sacred Wars, the gas theory and its evidence, Delphi's role in colonisation and politics); Dodona and the oak, the Siwa oracle, Trophonius, incubation at Epidaurus and the Asclepieia; the Eleusinian Mysteries as far as they can be reconstructed (the Lesser and Greater, the procession, the kykeon, the Telesterion, the hierophant, the promise of a better afterlife, the profanation scandal of 415); Orphism and the gold tablets, the Orphic Dionysus and the Titans, Pythagorean overlap; the Dionysiac mysteries and maenadism (and what Euripides' Bacchae does and does not tell us); the Samothracian mysteries and the Cabiri; and the Hellenistic and Roman import cults (Isis, Cybele, Mithras) as Greek-mediated. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

Households, women, slaves, the symposium and sexuality

The social facts the myths assume. Athens mostly, because that is where the evidence is.

UNIT 9

Art, the ground, and the afterlife of it all

How to read a temple and a vase, how to read a site, and how the whole thing reached you.

The material record is the second great source for myth. Learn its grammar.

9.3

Reception: how it all reached you

Byzantium, the Renaissance, Winckelmann, Freud, and the modern retellings that put the gaps in your knowledge in the first place.

9.4

Capstone: the whole thing in one sitting

The final exam. Run it only after everything above.

I have completed a 49-module course on Greek history and mythology covering sources, geography, the Bronze Age, Hesiod's cosmogony, the Olympians and lesser gods, the underworld, the heroes (Perseus, Heracles, Theseus, Jason, the Theban cycle, the secondary heroes, the metamorphoses), the full Trojan cycle including the Iliad, the lost Epic Cycle, the Odyssey and the house of Atreus, the Archaic age (the polis, colonisation, tyrants, Sparta, Athens to Cleisthenes, lyric and the Presocratics), the Classical age (Persian Wars, Athenian empire, Peloponnesian War, drama, philosophy, the fourth century, Alexander), the Hellenistic and Roman periods, religion and daily life, and art and reception. Give me a 40-question final examination that tests integration across modules rather than isolated facts: questions that require connecting a myth to the society that told it, a source to its bias, an event to its long consequences. Mix formats: 15 short-answer, 10 "explain the connection between X and Y", 10 "which source says this and why should we doubt it", 5 essay questions. Provide full model answers after the questions. Then list the ten topics where a reader of this course is most likely still to have gaps, with one follow-up prompt each.

UNIT 10

Deep dive I: the Trojan War

Optional extension after the core course, or after unit 4 if Troy is what you came for. Twelve modules that take the people, the poems, the place and the afterlife of the war to the depth the survey could not.

10.1

Achilles

The best of the Achaeans, across every source that touched him.

Give me an in-depth report on Achilles as a figure across the whole ancient tradition, assuming I already know the Iliad's plot. Cover: his birth and the Thetis prophecy (the son greater than his father, Prometheus' secret); the Styx-dipping versus the fire-and-ambrosia versions and which is early; Chiron and the education on Pelion; Scyros, Deidamia and Neoptolemus; the choice of two fates in Iliad 9 and what it implies about the heroic economy of kleos; Achilles and Patroclus (the Iliad's text, Aeschylus' Myrmidons, Plato's Symposium, Aeschines) and the ancient debate about the nature of the bond; his wrath as menis and its theology; Briseis; the Shield; the mutilation of Hector and the pity for Priam; his death in the Aethiopis, the heel in Statius and later; the funeral games and the Judgement of the Arms; his afterlife: the White Island (Leuke) cult, Elysium versus the Odyssey 11 shade who would rather be a living slave, Polyxena's sacrifice at his tomb; his reception: Alexander at Troy, the Achilleid of Statius, Achilles in Catullus 64, in Shakespeare's Troilus, in Miller's Song of Achilles. 10.8

The other Greeks at Troy

Diomedes, the two Ajaxes, Nestor, Patroclus, Antilochus, Idomeneus, Philoctetes, Neoptolemus, Teucer and the rest.

Give me an in-depth report on the secondary Greek heroes of the Trojan War, one profile each with sources and later fate. Cover: Diomedes (his aristeia in Book 5 including wounding Aphrodite and Ares, the exchange with Glaucus in Book 6, the Doloneia, the Palladium, his return to an unfaithful wife and his second life in Italy with cult in Apulia); Ajax son of Telamon (the duel with Hector, the defence of the ships, the shield like a tower, the Judgement of the Arms, the madness and suicide in Sophocles, his cult at Salamis and the Athenian tribe); Ajax son of Oileus (the rape of Cassandra, the shipwreck, the Locrian maidens' tribute to Athena at Troy as a historical ritual); Nestor (the garrulous counsellor, his Pylos and the Linear B palace, Nestor's cup from Pithekoussai); Patroclus and Antilochus as the two whose deaths define Achilles; Idomeneus and the Cretan contingent; Menelaus as warrior; Philoctetes (the bow of Heracles, Lemnos, Sophocles' play and its ethics, his Italian foundations); Neoptolemus (Scyros, the killing of Priam and Astyanax, Andromache, his death at Delphi and his cult there); Teucer and the founding of Cypriot Salamis; Calchas and the seers; Thersites as anti-hero. End with what the spread of these heroes' cults across the Greek and Italian world tells us about how the Trojan legend was used to found cities and claim land. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

10.9

The gods at Troy

Zeus' plan, the partisans, Thetis, the theomachy, and whether the gods are to blame.

From Zeno's painted colonnade to the teachers of Roman senators.

Give me an in-depth report on the Stoic school from Zeno to Posidonius, extending module 7.2, which outlines the physics and the ethics and Posidonius' quarrel with Chrysippus over the passions: point back to it and go deeper. Cover: Zeno of Citium, his Cynic training and his Republic; the Painted Stoa; Cleanthes and his Hymn to Zeus; Chrysippus' system in detail: the logic (propositions and the indemonstrable arguments), the physics of pneuma, tension and mixture, fate, providence and the conflagration, and the ethics of oikeiosis; Diogenes of Babylon and Antipater of Tarsus; the sources: von Arnim's Stoicorum Veterum Fragmenta, Diogenes Laertius book 7, Stobaeus, Cicero, and the polemics of Plutarch and Galen; and Panaetius and Posidonius as the hinge to Rome (module 7.3 has Panaetius in Scipio's house): hand off to Rome modules 13.1 and 13.2 for what Romans made of them. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
12.8

Sceptics and Neoplatonists: the late schools to 529

The doubters and the mystics, from Pyrrho to the last head of the Academy.

Give me an in-depth report on Greek scepticism and the later Platonists, from Pyrrho to AD 529, extending modules 7.2 and 7.4, which outline both, carry the disputes over Pyrrho, over theurgy and over the closing of the Academy, and already tell Hypatia's death and the journey of Damascius and Simplicius to Khusro's court and back in 532: point back to them and go deeper. Cover: Pyrrho and Timon; the Academy's sceptical turn (Arcesilaus, Carneades, Clitomachus, Philo of Larissa) and Antiochus of Ascalon's return to doctrine; Aenesidemus and Sextus Empiricus (Outlines of Pyrrhonism and the modes); Middle Platonism in a paragraph (Plutarch and Alcinous); Plotinus (the Enneads, Porphyry's Life of Plotinus), with the One, Intellect and Soul argued rather than listed; Porphyry, Iamblichus and theurgy, and Proclus; Hypatia as a philosopher (module 7.4 tells her death); Justinian's measure of 529 and the argument over what it did; and Damascius, Simplicius and the philosophers who went to the Persian court (Agathias 2.30–31). Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
UNIT 13

Quality audit

Not content but a check on content. Reports 10.4, 10.7 to 10.12 and all of units 11 and 12 were written with Opus 5.5; every other report so far with Fable 5.1. This unit audits unit 9 and the deep dives against a rubric calibrated on the core course, uses the Fable-written reports in that range as the control group, and takes in any report written later until the audit is run.

13.1

Calibrate the rubric

Turn the core course into a measuring stick before measuring anything with it.

I am auditing a self-study course in Greek history and mythology made of long reports written to fixed prompts. The four reports below, with the prompts they answer, are the quality baseline: 2.1 (Cosmogony), 4.2 (The Iliad), 6.3 (The Peloponnesian War) and 8.1 (Ritual practice). [Paste each prompt and report.]

Derive an evaluation rubric from them with these eight dimensions: (1) factual accuracy; (2) citation precision: author, work and book or line numbers that can be checked; (3) prompt coverage: every item the prompt names is addressed; (4) depth and argument: the report explains why, not only what; (5) handling of disputes: ancient variants and modern scholarly positions stated fairly, with names on each side; (6) self-test quality: questions test understanding rather than recall, and model answers are correct; (7) carry-forward usefulness; (8) house style: British spelling, no preamble, plain prose, body length within 15 percent of the target, valid structure.

For each dimension give a 1 to 5 scale with a concrete description of what a 1, 3 and 5 look like, anchored with short quoted examples from the baseline reports. Score the four baseline reports on the rubric so later audits are comparable. Output the rubric as a table, the baseline scores as a second table, and a blank scorecard template I can reuse for module 13.2.
report coming soon
13.2

Audit one report

Run once per report in scope. Produces a scorecard, a verdict and an errata table.

Using the rubric below [paste the rubric from 13.1], audit the report below [paste the prompt it answers, then the report]. Work through these steps and show the results of each.

1. Coverage: list every item the prompt names and mark it covered, thin or missing.
2. Fact-check: extract the 15 most checkable claims (dates, names, attributions, numbers, book and line citations) and verify each. Mark it correct, wrong (give the correction and the source) or unverifiable. Check every book or line citation you can.
3. Disputes: for each red-box disagreement, say whether the dispute is real and fairly stated with named sources or scholars on each side. List any important dispute the report leaves out.
4. Depth: name the three strongest and the three weakest passages and say why.
5. Self-test: answer each question yourself from the report alone. Flag any model answer that is wrong or ambiguous, and any question answerable without reading the report.
6. Style and structure: body word count against the target, British spelling, preamble or closing filler, mannered phrasing, broken markup.
7. Score every rubric dimension 1 to 5 with a one-line justification.

Verdict: pass (no dimension below 4 and no factual errors), revise (a list of specific edits fixes it) or rewrite (two or more dimensions below 3, or more than two factual errors). End with an errata table: location, current text, corrected text, source.
report coming soon
13.3

Deep-dive overlap check

A deep dive has to go deeper. This measures whether it did, against the survey module it extends.

Below are an earlier module and the deep-dive module that extends it. [Paste both, with their prompts.] The pairings to run are: 4.1 with 10.1, 10.2 and 10.5; 4.2 with 10.3 and 10.6; 4.4 with 10.4 and 10.7; 4.3 with 10.8; 2.2 with 10.9; 1.2 with 10.10; 1.3 with 10.11; 9.3 with 10.12; 6.6 with 11.1; 6.1 with 11.3; 6.7 with each of 11.2 and 11.4 to 11.8; 5.6, 2.1 and 8.2 with 12.1; 6.5 with 12.2 to 12.5; 8.4 with 12.3 and 12.6; 6.7, 11.5 and 11.7 with 12.4; 7.2 with 12.5 to 12.8; 7.3 with 12.7; and 7.4 with 12.8.

Go through the deep dive section by section and classify each as new material, deeper treatment of a point the survey made (say exactly what is added), or repetition. Estimate the share of the deep dive in each class. List every contradiction between the two reports and say which one is right, with the source. Verdict: the deep dive earns its place if at least 70 percent is new or deeper and there are no unresolved contradictions. If it fails, name the sections to cut and what should replace them.
report coming soon
13.4

Compare the cohorts

Did quality hold across the model change? One table and one sentence.

Below are the scorecards from module 13.2 for every audited report, each tagged with the model that wrote it. [Paste them.] The audit cohort is every report written with a model other than Fable 5.1: 10.4, 10.7 to 10.12 and all of units 11 and 12 (deep dive III), all with Opus 5.5, and any report added later. The control cohort is the reports written with Fable 5.1 (unit 9, and 10.1 to 10.3, 10.5 and 10.6).

For each rubric dimension give, per cohort, the mean, the range and the worst case. Give factual errors per 1,000 words, the average overshoot of the length target, and the share of pass, revise and rewrite verdicts. Say whether any difference between cohorts is larger than the variation inside the control cohort; if not, call it noise. Name any systematic pattern you can see, such as longer but thinner, more hedging, fewer line citations, weaker self-tests or more repetition of the survey modules. Conclude with one sentence answering: did quality hold? Then list the reports to fix, in priority order.

Fix a report

Apply the audit. Run once per report that came back revise or rewrite.

[Paste all three.] Produce a corrected version of the report that applies every erratum and every revise item, keeps every passage the audit marked as strong, stays within 10 percent of the target length, and keeps the same structure: headed sections, disagreement boxes, a ten-question self-test with answers, and three carry-forward ideas. After the report, list each change you made in one line, grouped by audit item.

Academy›History›Course 2 · Rome
COURSE 2 · ROME · Personal Mythology · IN PROGRESS

Roman history and mythology

Four parts and three deep dives, 79 modules, plus a quality audit. History and myth are interleaved more tightly than in the Greek course, because Rome told its myths as history: the legends sit inside the periods that produced them. Every module is a long-form report with named primary sources, the places where the accounts disagree, a ten-question self-test and three ideas to carry forward.

59of 79 reports written
11of 14 units complete
180kwords so far
13hours of reading
ContinueStart the course0.1 How we know anything: the sourcesSyllabusAll 85 prompts, copyable

1,500 years, to scale

1000 BC → AD 476
1000 BC509 BCAD 1476

The late Republic that most people mean by "ancient Rome" is the thin red slice: about a century. The kings in the gold band were written about by Romans living in it, five hundred years after the fact. The eastern empire that kept the Roman name until 1453 runs off the right-hand edge.

  • Iron Age Italy1000 BC – 753 BCmodules 1.1 and 1.2
  • Regal period753 BC – 509 BCmodules 1.3 to 1.6, all of it legend written down five centuries later
  • Early and middle Republic509 BC – 133 BCunits 3 and 4
  • Late Republic133 BC – 27 BCunit 5 and deep dive 11
  • Principate27 BC – AD 284units 6 and 7, and module 8.1
  • Late empireAD 284 – AD 476unit 8; the eastern empire runs on to 1453

How to read it

five rules
  1. Parts I to IV in order. Each report assumes the ones before it. Part I matters more here than in Greece: the evidence for Rome's first four centuries is late and partisan, and every later report assumes you know how late. Part IV ends with a capstone exam.
  2. Deep dives when you want them. Unit 11 (the fall of the Republic) extends unit 5; unit 12 (the Aeneid) extends module 6.3 and, for Aeneas, module 1.3; unit 13 (Roman philosophy) extends modules 4.5, 5.6, 7.3 and 10.2. Take each straight after the material it extends, or after the capstone.
  3. Greece behind you. Rome points back to the Greek course instead of repeating it: Greek 7.3 and 7.4 for the Greek side of the conquest, 2.2 for the Olympians, 9.3 and 10.12 for reception. Open the Greek module when a report names it.
  4. One or two a sitting, self-test before moving on. Under 7 of 10, re-read. The carry-forward ideas are the ones later reports lean on.
  5. A study text, not scripture. Citations are given so you can check them against the primary sources. Where sources disagree, the report says so in a red-edged box.
I
Units 0–214 modulesc. 1000 to 509 BC, and the gods throughout

Ground rules and origins

Where the evidence comes from, the land and peoples of Italy, the legends Rome told about its birth, and the religion everything after it ran on.

UNIT 0FoundationscompleteThree modules that fix the evidence, the land and the dates before any story is told. Rome is a harder case than Greece: its early history was written down centuries late, by Romans with something to prove.

The Republic

From the expulsion of the kings to Actium: the conquest of Italy, Carthage and the East, then a century of reformers, generals and civil war.

UNIT 3The early Republic and the conquest of Italy, 509 to 264 BCcompleteFrom a city-state fighting its neighbours to master of the peninsula. Livy's first decade is both the main source and the main problem.

The Empire

Augustus and the Principate, the third-century crisis and the Christian empire, to the last emperor in the West and the eastern empire that outlived him.

UNIT 6Augustus, 31 BC to AD 14completeThe man who ended the Republic while claiming to restore it, and the poets who gave his regime its myths.

The reading spine

primary and secondary

The reports keep naming these. Read the primary ones at least once each, in this order, any decent translation: Livy, books 1 to 5 and then 21 to 30 · Polybius, books 1 to 6 · Virgil, Aeneid (book 8 with module 1.3) · Ovid, Fasti, then Metamorphoses · Sallust, The War with Catiline · Caesar, The Gallic War · Cicero, selected letters (Shackleton Bailey's translation) and the Second Philippic · Plutarch's Roman Lives as each name comes up · Augustus, Res Gestae · Tacitus, Annals · Suetonius, The Twelve Caesars · Seneca, Letters to Lucilius (a selection), Epictetus, Handbook, and Marcus Aurelius, Meditations (with deep dive 13) · Ammianus Marcellinus, books 14 to 31.

Secondary, three only: T. J. Cornell, The Beginnings of Rome; Mary Beard, John North and Simon Price, Religions of Rome; Mary Beard, SPQR, with Peter Heather, The Fall of the Roman Empire.

Academy›Rome›Syllabus
ROME · SYLLABUS · 85 modules · every prompt in full

Rome: syllabus and prompts

This is the complete syllabus the reports were generated from. Each module has its drop-in prompt; copy it into a new chat to regenerate a report yourself, extend one, or compare a fresh answer against the one here. Where a report exists it is linked.

How to run it

  1. Do the modules in order. Each prompt assumes the ones before it, and the Greek course before that: where Rome touches Greece, the prompt points back to the Greek module instead of repeating it.
  2. Tap copy prompt, paste the prompt into a new chat here, read the report, do the self-test at the end. If you score under 7/10, re-read before moving on.
  3. Tick the box when done. Progress is stored on this phone only.
  4. Every prompt asks for named primary sources and for the places where the accounts disagree. For early Rome that matters more than it did for Greece: almost everything before 300 BC reached us through writers of the first century BC, and the question is always what they could have known.

Units 11, 12 and 13 are deep dives: optional, and best taken after the core material they extend (unit 5; module 6.3; modules 4.5, 5.6, 7.3 and 10.2) or after the capstone.

Honest scoping: no syllabus gets you all of it. Rome left more words than Greece and fewer stories, and its stories come disguised as history. What this gets you is the skeleton, the main sources read in the right order, and enough structure that anything you meet later snaps into place.

UNIT 0

Foundations

Three modules that fix the evidence, the land and the dates before any story is told. Rome is a harder case than Greece: its early history was written down centuries late, by Romans with something to prove.

0.1

How we know anything: the sources

Livy wrote about Romulus seven centuries after the fact. Know what that means before you read a word of him.

Give me an in-depth report on the sources for Roman history and mythology, with the emphasis on the problem that defines the subject: Rome's early history was written down centuries after the events, by Romans with agendas. Cover: the first Roman historians, who wrote in Greek (Fabius Pictor, writing around 200 BC; Cincius Alimentus) and why they did; Cato's Origines and the Latin annalists (Cassius Hemina, Calpurnius Piso, Valerius Antias, Claudius Quadrigarius, Licinius Macer), known only in fragments, and the charge that they invented; the raw material they claimed to use (the Annales Maximi of the pontiffs, the lists of consuls and triumphs, family archives, funeral orations) and the complaints Cicero and Livy themselves make about it; the surviving narratives: Polybius (the outsider's analysis), Livy (Ab urbe condita, 142 books of which 35 survive, and his preface on legend), Dionysius of Halicarnassus (Roman Antiquities, and his thesis that Rome was a Greek city), Diodorus, Plutarch's Roman Lives, Appian and Cassius Dio for the later Republic; the antiquarians (Varro, Verrius Flaccus through Festus, Aulus Gellius, Macrobius) for religion and custom; and the imperial historians in outline (Velleius, Tacitus, Suetonius, the Historia Augusta, Ammianus). Material sources: inscriptions (the Lapis Niger, the Duenos vase, the Satricum stone, the Scipio epitaphs, the Res Gestae, Claudius' speech on the Lyon tablet), coins as propaganda, the Pyrgi tablets, and archaeology in the Forum and on the Palatine. Then the method: the hypercritical tradition from Niebuhr and Mommsen to Pais and Forsythe, the more trusting positions of Cornell and Carandini, Wiseman's view of the early stories as dramatic inventions, and how to judge a story first found in Livy. End with the main traps: treating Livy book 1 as history, treating Dionysius as independent of Livy, trusting the Historia Augusta, and reading Roman myth only through Ovid. Point back to Greek module 0.1 for the Greek sources rather than repeating them. Format: about 3000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report

Italy and the Mediterranean: the shape of Rome's world

A city on a river crossing, between Etruria and Campania, in the middle of a peninsula in the middle of a sea.

0.3

The timeline spine, 1000 BC to AD 476

About eighteen dates to hang everything on, and the eastern empire that kept going to 1453.

UNIT 1

Italy before Rome and the founding legends

The peoples Rome grew out of and the stories it told about its birth. Archaeology and legend are read side by side, because for this period neither can be read alone.

1.1

The peoples of Iron Age Italy

Villanovans, Latins, Sabines, Samnites and Greek colonists: the crowded peninsula Rome came out of.

Give me an in-depth report on the peoples of Italy in the Iron Age, c. 1000 to 500 BC, the world Rome emerged from. Cover: the Final Bronze Age background (the Terramare, the Proto-Villanovan cremation cemeteries) and the Villanovan culture of Etruria and Bologna (biconical urns, hut urns, the shift to inhumation, the rise of a warrior elite); the Latial culture and its phases, the early cemeteries of Rome (the Forum burials, the Esquiline) and of the Alban Hills, and Osteria dell'Osa near Gabii as the best-excavated Latin community; the Latins as a people and their shared cults (the Latin festival on the Alban Mount, Diana at Aricia, the thirteen altars at Lavinium) and the Latin League; the Sabines, the Oscan- and Umbrian-speaking peoples (the Samnites, the Iguvine Tables as a window on Umbrian religion), the Veneti, Ligurians, Messapians and Picenes; the languages of Italy, Indo-European and not, and what the spread of the alphabet shows; the Greek colonies of the south (Pithekoussai and Cumae, then Magna Graecia and Sicily) seen from the Italian side, with the Nestor cup and the first writing in Latium; and the Phoenicians in the west. Point back to Greek module 5.2 for Greek colonisation itself. End with the Sabines as the first case of the course's method: what archaeology and language allow us to say about a people that Roman legend (module 1.4) made into Rome's co-founders. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

The Etruscans

Rome's teachers and first foreign kings, whose language we can read aloud but barely understand.

UNIT 2

The gods of Rome

Roman religion was a system of obligations, not a body of stories. Learn how it worked before the Republic, which ran on it, and before the Greek gods it absorbed.

2.1

What Roman religion was

Pax deorum, ritual exactness, and why the Romans are said to have had no mythology.

Give me an in-depth report on the nature of Roman religion. Cover: religio and superstitio, and why 'belief' is the wrong question; the pax deorum and the contractual logic of do ut des, vows and their fulfilment; ritual exactness and instauratio, the repetition of a flawed rite; the state as the religious actor and magistrates as officiants; prodigies and their expiation as Livy records them year by year; Varro's three theologies (mythical, natural, civil) as Augustine preserves them in City of God book 6, and Varro's claim that Rome worshipped without images for 170 years; numen, the 'animism' theory and its collapse; Dumézil's three functions and the archaic triad of Jupiter, Mars and Quirinus; the old claim that the Romans had no mythology of their own and the modern answers to it (Dumézil's myths turned into history, Wiseman's lost dramatic myths, Feeney's Literature and Religion at Rome, Beard, North and Price's Religions of Rome); and how religion and politics were one activity, from augury in elections to Cicero's On the Nature of the Gods and On Divination. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

Carthage and the Mediterranean, 264 to 133 BC

Hannibal, Polybius' fifty-three years, and the moment Rome became an empire, by its own account without meaning to.

Carthage and Dido

The rival city, its gods and its queen, before Virgil made her Aeneas' victim.

Twenty-three years at sea for Sicily, and Rome's first province.

UNIT 5

The fall of the Republic, 133 to 30 BC

A century of reformers, generals and civil wars that ended with one man in charge. The best-documented century of antiquity, because Cicero kept his letters.

Land, tribunes and the first political killings in the Forum.

Law, army and daily life

The institutions that outlasted the emperors, and the social facts the stories assume.

9.1

Roman law

From the Twelve Tables to Justinian's Digest: Rome's most durable invention.

Give me an in-depth report on Roman law from the Twelve Tables to Justinian. Cover: ius, fas and mos; the Twelve Tables and the pontiffs' interpretation; the praetor's edict and the formulary system; the classical jurists (Quintus Mucius Scaevola, Labeo, Gaius, Papinian, Ulpian, Paul), and Gaius' Institutes as the one classical textbook to survive, found in a Verona palimpsest in 1816; persons, things and actions; paternal power, slavery and manumission in law; citizenship and its grades; criminal law and its brutal penalties (the sack for parricide, crucifixion, condemnation to the arena); the Theodosian Code of 438 and Justinian's Corpus of Civil Law (Code, Digest, Institutes, Novels); and the revival at Bologna and the civil-law world that descends from it. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
9.2

The army

Legion, camp, discipline and the soldier's life, from Polybius to Vindolanda.

Give me an in-depth report on the Roman army from the manipular legion to the late empire. Cover: the citizen militia and the census classes; the manipular legion in Polybius book 6 (hastati, principes, triarii, velites), extending module 3.4's outline of it against the phalanx, the camp, decimation and the rewards for valour; the changes associated with Marius and the cohort legion; the Augustan professional army (module 6.4 gives its establishment: point back), pay, length of service, the auxiliaries and citizenship on discharge (the military diplomas); the soldier's life from the Vindolanda tablets and tombstones; equipment and engineering; the triumph as ritual; the late army of field and frontier troops; and the army as a political actor from Sulla to the soldier emperors. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
9.3

Family, women and slaves

The paterfamilias, marriage, the real power of women, slavery and the freedmen.

Give me an in-depth report on the Roman family, women and slavery. Cover: the familia and the paterfamilias, paternal power and its real limits; marriage with and without manus, divorce and dowry; age at marriage and the demography (Saller and Shaw); Roman women in public and private, from Cornelia and Hortensia's speech of 42 BC to Livia and the imperial women, the 'Laudatio Turiae', and the gap between law and practice; childhood, wet nurses and schooling; slavery: numbers, sources (war, birth, trade, exposure), work, the agricultural gangs and household slaves, the slave wars in Sicily and Spartacus (module 5.3 tells his war: a sentence here), manumission and the freedmen as a class, with Petronius' Trimalchio and the epitaphs; and how the modern study of the Roman family has changed (Treggiari, Dixon, Bradley). Point back to Greek module 8.5 for the Greek comparison. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

9.4

Bread, baths and games

Tenements, aqueducts, the grain dole, gladiators and the circus.

Give me an in-depth report on life in the city of Rome. Cover: population estimates; housing (the house and the tenement block, fire and collapse, Juvenal's Satire 3); water: the aqueducts and Frontinus' On Aqueducts; the grain supply, Ostia and the dole; patronage and the morning salutation; the baths as a social institution; food and the dinner party; the games: gladiators (origins, types, the training schools, the munus, epitaphs, the Colosseum's machinery), chariot racing and the colours at the Circus Maximus, wild-beast hunts, and executions as spectacle; the theatre and pantomime; Juvenal's 'bread and circuses' (10.81) and what it gets wrong; and Pompeii and Herculaneum as the town-level check on all of this. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

9.5

Reading a site: Rome on foot

The Forum, Palatine, Capitoline, Colosseum and the museums as a walkable exam.

Give me an in-depth report that turns the city of Rome into a walkable exam on the course. Build it as a route: the Capitoline (the temple platform, the Tabularium, the Capitoline Museums with the wolf, the equestrian Marcus Aurelius and the Fasti); the Forum (the Lapis Niger, the Regia, the temple of Vesta and the House of the Vestals, the Curia, the Rostra, the temples of Saturn, Castor and the deified Julius, the basilicas, the arches of Septimius Severus and Titus); the Palatine (the Iron Age huts, the houses of Augustus and Livia, the Flavian palace); the Imperial Fora, Trajan's Markets and Column; the Colosseum and the Arch of Constantine; the Circus Maximus, the Forum Boarium and Sant'Omobono; the Campus Martius (Pantheon, Ara Pacis, Mausoleum of Augustus, Largo Argentina); the Baths of Caracalla; and the National Museum at Palazzo Massimo for the garden room from Livia's villa and the bronze Boxer. For each stop: what is there, which module it belongs to, and the one thing to look for. Point back to Greek module 9.2, which does the same for Athens. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

UNIT 10

Literature, myth-making and reception

How Rome rewrote Greek myth, how it read its own past in poetry and stone, and how all of it reached you.

10.1

Ovid and the Roman remaking of Greek myth

The book through which Europe learned Greek myth, read as the Roman poem it is.

Give me an in-depth report on Ovid's Metamorphoses as the book through which later Europe learned Greek myth, read as the Roman poem it is. Cover: its structure from Chaos to the apotheosis of Caesar; the Roman frame (the council of the gods as the Senate, Jupiter and Augustus, the ending on Caesar's star); which stories Ovid invented or transformed (Echo and Narcissus, Pyramus and Thisbe, Arachne, Pygmalion, Medusa) and which he took from lost Hellenistic poets; the Roman names and why they stuck; the Fasti as its counterpart for Roman myth; the Heroides; and the poem's later life in the Ovide moralisé, Chaucer, Shakespeare, Titian and Bernini. Point back to Greek modules 0.1 and 3.7, which treat Ovid as a trap for the Greek material, and concentrate on what he did as a Roman. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
10.2

Imperial literature, Seneca to Apuleius

Tragedy, anti-epic, satire, the novel and history under the emperors.

Give me an in-depth report on Latin literature under the emperors, AD 14 to c. 200, with a coda on late antiquity. Cover: the 'Silver Latin' label and why to drop it; Seneca's tragedies (deep dive module 13.5 takes his life and philosophy); Lucan's anti-epic on the civil war; Petronius' Satyricon and Trimalchio's dinner; Persius and Juvenal; Martial; the Flavian epic poets (Statius' Thebaid and Silvae, Silius Italicus' Punica, Valerius Flaccus' Argonautica), whom module 7.2 places at court; the elder and younger Pliny; Quintilian on the orator; Tacitus as historian and stylist; Suetonius and the biographical tradition; Apuleius' Golden Ass as the one complete Latin novel; and a short coda on late Latin (Ausonius, Claudian, Ammianus, Jerome's Vulgate, Augustine's Confessions) as the bridge to the Middle Ages. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
10.3

Reading Roman art and architecture

Portraits, reliefs, wall painting, the arch and the dome: the grammar of the material record.

Give me an in-depth report on how to read Roman art and architecture. Cover: the Etruscan and Greek inheritance; the republican portrait and 'verism', ancestor masks and the Barberini Togatus; copies of Greek sculpture and what 'Roman copy' means; historical relief from the Ara Pacis (module 6.2 reads its programme) to the Arch of Titus and the spiral friezes of the columns of Trajan and Marcus; the four Pompeian styles of wall painting and the Villa of the Mysteries; mosaic; architecture: concrete, the arch and vault, the temple on a podium, the basilica, the bath, the amphitheatre, the Pantheon's dome; the imperial portrait as propaganda and the erasure of the condemned; sarcophagi and myth in funerary art; and late antique style (the Tetrarchs, the Arch of Constantine) as change rather than decline. Point back to Greek module 9.1 for the Greek grammar this builds on. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

Reception: how Rome reached you

Charlemagne, Petrarch, Machiavelli, Gibbon, Mussolini, Hollywood. Read with Greek 9.3.

Give me an in-depth report on the reception of Rome from late antiquity to now. Cover: Rome as an idea after 476 (the papacy, Charlemagne and the Holy Roman Empire, the Marvels of the City of Rome and Virgil the magician, Dante's Virgil); Petrarch, Cola di Rienzo and the Renaissance of Rome; Machiavelli's Discourses on Livy and the republican tradition; Shakespeare's Roman plays; Gibbon; the American founders, the French Revolution's Brutus and Napoleon's empire; nineteenth-century archaeology and Mommsen; Mussolini's romanità, the Via dell'Impero and the Augustan exhibition of 1937; Rome on screen (Quo Vadis, Ben-Hur, Spartacus, I, Claudius, Gladiator, HBO's Rome) and in Asterix and the novels from Graves to Harris; and the current scholarly and public arguments about who owns Rome. Point back to Greek modules 9.3 and 10.12 and do not repeat them. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
10.5

Capstone: the whole thing in one sitting

The final exam. Run it only after everything above.

I have completed a 54-module course on Roman history and mythology covering sources, geography and chronology; the peoples of Iron Age Italy and the Etruscans; the founding legends (Evander, Hercules and Aeneas, Romulus and Remus, the seven kings, Lucretia and Brutus); Roman religion (its nature, the gods and their Greek doubles, priests and calendar, household and the dead, the foreign cults); the early Republic and the conquest of Italy; Carthage and the Punic Wars; the conquest of the Mediterranean and its cultural price; the fall of the Republic from the Gracchi to Actium; Augustus and the Augustan writers; the Principate from Tiberius to the Severans; the third-century crisis, Diocletian, Constantine and the Christian empire; the fall of the West and the eastern empire to 1453; law, the army, family and slavery, the city and the games; Ovid, imperial literature, Roman art and reception. Give me a 40-question final examination that tests integration across modules rather than isolated facts: questions that require connecting a legend to the period that wrote it down, a source to its bias, an institution to its long consequences, and Rome to the Greek course that came before it. Mix formats: 15 short-answer, 10 "explain the connection between X and Y", 10 "which source says this and why should we doubt it", 5 essay questions. Provide full model answers after the questions. Then list the ten topics where a reader of this course is most likely still to have gaps, with one follow-up prompt each.
read the report
UNIT 11

Deep dive I: the fall of the Republic

Optional extension after unit 5, or after the capstone. Eight modules that take the institutions, the people, the sources and the explanations to the depth the survey could not.

11.1

How the Republic worked at its height

Elections, the Senate's authority and the long argument over whether Rome was a democracy.

Give me an in-depth report on how the Roman Republic actually worked at its height, c. 200 to 133 BC, extending modules 3.1 and 4.5. Cover: the magistracies and the cursus honorum after the lex Villia annalis of 180 BC; elections in the centuriate and tribal assemblies, the voting order and what it did to the result; the Senate's authority without legal power, its decrees and the leader of the Senate; nobles and new men, and the competitive aristocratic ethic of glory and standing; patronage and clientship and the modern doubts about how far they decided votes; electoral bribery and the laws against it; the role of the contio; and the debate begun by Fergus Millar in the 1980s over whether Rome was a kind of democracy, with the replies of North, Mouritsen, Hölkeskamp and Morstein-Marx. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
11.2

Generals and their armies

The 'Marian reforms', the veterans, the land, and whose army it was.

Give me an in-depth report on the relationship between Roman generals and their armies from Marius to Octavian, extending modules 5.2 to 5.5 and 9.2. Cover: the property qualification and the recruitment of the poorest citizens, and the modern doubt over whether Marius changed recruitment as much as the textbooks say; long commands and provinces as bases of power; the veteran problem and land settlement; the soldiers' oath, donatives and loyalty to the general rather than the state; the marches on Rome of 88, 83 to 82, 49 and 43 BC and how each army was persuaded; Caesar and his legions (the Tenth, the mutinies of 49 and 47); triumphs, salutations as imperator, and the ideology of victory; and the argument over whether the 'client army' explains the fall of the Republic (Gabba, Brunt, Keaveney, de Blois). Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report

Cover: the urban plebs and its size; the contio as the place where politicians spoke to the people; the tribunate as an instrument; the grain laws from Gaius Gracchus to Clodius' free grain of 58 BC; the neighbourhood associations (collegia), their banning and revival; Clodius and Milo, the gangs, the burning of the Senate house in 52 BC and Cicero's Pro Milone; the Senate's 'final decree' as legalised violence; the Italian voters after 89 BC; and the scholarly argument over whether the people were actors or an audience (Millar, Mouritsen, Morstein-Marx, Lintott's Violence in Republican Rome, Tatum on Clodius). Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

11.8

Why the Republic fell

Sallust, Appian and Dio; Mommsen, Syme, Gruen and Flower. The ancient and modern answers.

Give me an in-depth report on the ancient and modern explanations for the fall of the Roman Republic, extending unit 5 and this deep dive. Cover: the ancient answers (Sallust's moral decline after the removal of the fear of Carthage, Polybius' cycle of constitutions, Lucan's and Appian's civil-war narratives, Cassius Dio's view that monarchy was inevitable for an empire); the modern answers (Mommsen's Caesar as necessary saviour, Syme's oligarchy in The Roman Revolution, Gruen's The Last Generation of the Roman Republic arguing it was not dying, Brunt's social conflicts, Meier's 'crisis without alternative', Flower's sequence of republics, and recent arguments about Italy, the empire and the army); and a final assessment of which explanations survive. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.

UNIT 13

Deep dive III: Roman philosophy, the Stoics and Marcus Aurelius

Optional extension after modules 7.3 and 10.2, or after the capstone. Philosophy reached Rome as an embassy and a scandal and became the inner life of its governing class. Eight modules on how Romans used the Greek schools, above all the Stoa, ending with the emperor who wrote philosophy to himself.

13.1

Philosophy comes to Rome

Expulsions, embassies and house philosophers: how the Greek schools got into Roman households.

Give me an in-depth report on how Greek philosophy reached Rome and what Romans did with it, from the second century BC to Augustus, extending modules 4.5 and 5.6 and Greek module 7.2. Module 4.5 already covers the decree of 161 BC against philosophers and rhetors, the burning of the books of Numa in 181 and the embassy of 155 with Carneades' two speeches: point back to it and give each a sentence. Cover: the other measures and what they show (the censors' edict of 92 BC, which declared the schools of the new Latin rhetors displeasing but expelled no one, in Suetonius, On Grammarians and Rhetoricians 25 and Gellius 15.11.2; Athenaeus 12.547a on two Epicureans expelled in the consulship of a Lucius Postumius); Panaetius in the household of Scipio Aemilianus (module 4.5 already places him there, with Velleius 1.13.3 and the dispute over the 'Scipionic circle': point back and keep to what he taught Romans); Posidonius at Rhodes and his Roman visitors, Cicero as a student and Pompey as a general; Greek philosophers in Roman houses (Philodemus with Piso belongs to module 13.3): the blind Stoic Diodotus who lived and died in Cicero's house, Athenodorus Cordylion with Cato and Athenodorus son of Sandon with Augustus (Strabo 14.5.14 keeps the two apart), Arius Didymus at Augustus' court; Athens and Rhodes as the finishing school of a Roman education; Cicero's Latin philosophy only in outline (module 5.6 covers the works and his reasons for writing them: point back); why Romans took the ethics and left the theory, and what Roman suspicion of philosophy was about; and, as a later witness, Tacitus, Agricola 4, on a mother who curbed her son's zeal for philosophy beyond what a Roman senator should allow himself. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
13.2

Stoicism made Roman

Virtue, roles and providence: the Greek system as Romans received it and bent it to public life.

Give me an in-depth report on Stoic doctrine as Romans received and reshaped it, extending Greek module 7.2, which already outlines the system (virtue the only good, the indifferents, the passions as false judgements, Chrysippus' cylinder in Cicero, On Fate 42–43, the cosmic city): point back to it, and to Greek module 12.7 for the school from Zeno to Chrysippus, instead of restating them. Cover: the preferred and dispreferred indifferents as the space in which a public career happens; Hierocles' circles of concern as a Roman-era statement of oikeiosis (Greek module 12.7 gives Chrysippus' doctrine); the passions and their therapy; providence and the good man's suffering (Seneca, On Providence), and Seneca's two commonwealths (On Leisure 4.1) as the Roman form of the cosmic city; fate and responsibility as Romans argued them (Cicero, On Fate; Gellius 7.2; Seneca, Letters 107.11, his Latin of Cleanthes on the fates that lead the willing and drag the unwilling), leaving Chrysippus' compatibilism to Greek module 12.7; Panaetius' four personae and Cicero's On Duties as an ethic of role and office; the sage, the person making progress and the Stoic paradoxes; and the critics: Cicero mocking Cato's paradoxes before a jury (Pro Murena 61–66) and Horace's satires on the Stoic sage (Satires 1.3 and 2.3). Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
read the report
13.3

The rival schools at Rome

Epicureans, Academics, Pythagoreans and Cynics: the other choices, and why the senators chose the Stoa.

Give me an in-depth report on the philosophical schools other than the Stoa at Rome, from the late Republic to the Antonines, extending module 5.6 (Lucretius, Amafinius, Cicero's dialogues, Philodemus under Piso's patronage and Cassius as an Epicurean, each in a sentence there: point back and go deeper) and Greek modules 7.2 and 7.4. Cover: the Epicureans beside and after Lucretius: Philodemus, Piso and the library of the Villa of the Papyri at Herculaneum, Cassius the tyrannicide, Atticus, and Horace's 'pig from Epicurus' herd' (Epistles 1.4.16); the New Academy, Cicero the sceptic and what his scepticism allowed him to do; Antiochus of Ascalon's 'Old Academy', which Varro and Brutus followed; the Peripatetics, and Cratippus as teacher of Cicero's son at Athens; Nigidius Figulus, the Sextii and the Neopythagoreans; the Cynics under the empire: Demetrius, friend of Seneca and Thrasea, and Lucian's On the Death of Peregrinus, on the Cynic who burned himself at Olympia in 165 (Greek module 7.4 names the work only, so tell it here); why the Stoa won the senatorial class; and what Marcus Aurelius' endowed chairs at Athens in 176 (recorded in Greek module 7.4) show about the schools' standing. Greek modules 12.5, 12.6 and 12.8 take the Greek schools themselves. Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
next session
13.4

Cato and the Stoic opposition

A suicide as an argument: Cato's afterlife and the senators who died with him as their model.

Give me an in-depth report on Cato the Younger as a Stoic life and on the senators of the first century AD who made him their model, extending modules 5.4, 7.1 and 7.2. Modules 7.1 and 7.2 tell these reigns: point back for the politics and keep to the philosophy and the exemplary deaths. Cover: Cato as Plutarch's Cato Minor draws him, and his Stoic teachers, Antipater of Tyre and Athenodorus Cordylion (module 13.1 introduces Athenodorus: a sentence here); Utica only as a pointer to module 5.4, which tells the death; Cato after death: Cicero's Cato and Caesar's Anticato (modules 5.4 and 5.6 name them in a sentence each: tell the pamphlet war here, with Plutarch, Caesar 54), Seneca's Cato, Lucan's in books 2 and 9 (module 10.2 takes Lucan as a poet); Thrasea Paetus, his Life of Cato (used by Plutarch) and his death in 66, where the Annals break off (Tacitus, Annals 16.21–35); Arria's 'Paete, non dolet' (Pliny, Letters 3.16); Helvidius Priscus against Vespasian (Suetonius, Vespasian 15; Epictetus, Discourses 1.2.19–24); the expulsions of philosophers under Vespasian and Domitian; Arulenus Rusticus and Herennius Senecio killed for praising Thrasea and Helvidius (Tacitus, Agricola 2); and the debate over whether there was a 'Stoic opposition' at all (MacMullen, Enemies of the Roman Order, 1966; Brunt, 'Stoicism and the Principate', 1975). Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
next session
13.5

Seneca

Exile, the young Nero, a vast fortune and a staged death: the philosopher as minister.

Give me an in-depth report on the life and philosophy of Seneca the Younger, extending module 7.1, which tells Nero's reign, and module 10.2, which covers his tragedies. Cover: Corduba and the elder Seneca; illness, Egypt and the start of a career; exile to Corsica, 41–49, and the Consolation to Polybius; recall by Agrippina and the tutorship of Nero; minister beside Burrus, and On Clemency; wealth and the charge of hypocrisy (Suillius in Tacitus, Annals 13.42; Dio 61.10), with On the Happy Life as his answer; withdrawal in 62 (Annals 14.52–56); the works: the Moral Letters to Lucilius, the dialogues (On Anger, On the Shortness of Life, On Tranquillity of Mind, On the Happy Life), On Benefits and Natural Questions; his Stoicism, his use of Epicurus and the practice of nightly self-examination (On Anger 3.36); the death in 65 as a Socratic performance (Annals 15.60–64); Quintilian's verdict on his style (10.1.125–131); and the Christian Seneca: the forged letters to Paul and Tertullian's 'often ours' (On the Soul 20). The satire on Claudius' deification belongs to module 7.1 and the tragedies to module 10.2. Format: about 3000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
next session
13.6

Musonius Rufus and Epictetus

An exiled knight and a lame freedman: Stoicism taught as a way to live.

Give me an in-depth report on Musonius Rufus and Epictetus, extending module 13.2. Cover: Musonius' lectures as a pupil recorded them and Stobaeus preserved them: that women should study philosophy, that daughters should be educated as sons are, marriage, food and simple living; his exile after the Pisonian conspiracy (Tacitus, Annals 15.71), to Gyaros (Philostratus, Life of Apollonius 7.16); Epictetus: slave of Nero's freedman Epaphroditus, lame, Musonius' pupil, freed, expelled from Rome with the philosophers under Domitian (Gellius 15.11; module 13.4 tells the expulsions), his school at Nicopolis; Arrian's Discourses (four of eight books survive) and Handbook, and how much of the voice is Arrian's; what is up to us and what is not (Handbook 1); prohairesis, impressions and assent; roles and duties; the Cynic as Epictetus imagines him (Discourses 3.22); and his reach: the Handbook was adapted for Christian monks, modern cognitive therapy claims him, and Marcus Aurelius read him (module 13.7 tells Rusticus' gift of the notes, Meditations 1.7, and module 13.8 weighs the debt). Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
next session
13.7

Marcus Aurelius: a life

The emperor trained for philosophy who spent his reign at war.

Give me an in-depth report on the life of Marcus Aurelius, extending module 7.3, which already gives the adoptions of 138, the joint accession with Lucius Verus, the Parthian war, the plague with Galen's description and the dispute over its toll, the invasion of Italy and the auction of the palace furnishings, and the column: point back to it for each and spend the words on the man. Cover: his birth in 121 as Marcus Annius Verus and his family; Hadrian's nickname for him, 'Verissimus'; the adoptions of 138; marriage to Faustina the Younger and their children; the long apprenticeship under Antoninus (Meditations 1.16); his teachers: Fronto, whose letters Angelo Mai recovered from palimpsests from 1815, Herodes Atticus (point back to Greek module 7.4), and Junius Rusticus and the turn to philosophy (Meditations 1.7); accession in 161 and the partnership with Lucius Verus as Marcus lived it; the years at Carnuntum and Sirmium, with the wars themselves only in outline; the rain miracle on the column against the Christian versions of it; Avidius Cassius' revolt of 175, the rumours about Faustina and her death at Halala; Commodus made Augustus in 177; the martyrs of Lyon in 177 (Eusebius, Church History 5.1); and his death in 180. Sources: the Historia Augusta's Marcus, Dio's epitomators, Herodian book 1, Fronto's letters, the column and the equestrian statue. Modern biography: Birley, Marcus Aurelius (1966, revised 1987). Format: about 3000 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
next session
13.8

The Meditations and the afterlife of Roman Stoicism

Notes to himself in Greek, nearly lost, then a handbook for Europe.

Give me an in-depth report on Marcus Aurelius' Meditations and the later life of Roman Stoicism, extending modules 13.6 and 13.7. Cover: the text: twelve books in Greek, under the manuscripts' title To Himself, probably not his own; the two place notes, 'among the Quadi on the Granua' between books 1 and 2 and 'at Carnuntum' between books 2 and 3, and what they show about when and where he wrote; date and purpose: a private notebook or a set of spiritual exercises (Brunt, 'Marcus Aurelius in his Meditations', 1974; R. B. Rutherford, The Meditations of Marcus Aurelius: A Study, 1989; Pierre Hadot, The Inner Citadel); book 1 as a catalogue of debts, read as a whole (module 13.7 uses 1.7 and 1.16 for the life); the themes: impermanence, the view from above, the inner citadel, duty, the cosmic city, death; the debt to Epictetus; the transmission: Arethas about 900, the Suda, and the first edition of 1558–59 with Xylander's Latin translation, printed from a manuscript now lost; the afterlife: Justus Lipsius' neo-Stoicism (On Constancy, 1584), Montaigne, Frederick the Great, Matthew Arnold's essay of 1863, and modern popular Stoicism; and the question the book leaves: was its author a philosopher, or a tired emperor talking himself into his duty? Format: about 2500 words, headed sections, name sources, flag disagreements, end with a 10-question self-test with answers and the three ideas I must carry forward.
next session
UNIT 14

Quality audit

Not content but a check on content. Every report in this course so far was written with Opus 5.5 and is flagged for review with Fable 5.1; the syllabus itself was reviewed under 14.6 on 27 October 2026. Run this unit with Fable 5.1: it adapts the rubric Greek module 13.1 calibrates on the Greek core, audits each Rome report against it, checks the deep dives and the cross-references back to Greece for repetition, compares Rome with the Greek baseline, and reviews the syllabus itself.

14.1

Calibrate the rubric for Rome

Reuse the Greek rubric, add the three checks Rome needs, and score two samples before measuring anything.

I am auditing the Rome course of a self-study compendium on history and mythology. It follows a Greek course whose quality baseline is reports 2.1 (Cosmogony), 4.2 (The Iliad), 6.3 (The Peloponnesian War) and 8.1 (Ritual practice), and whose rubric was built in Greek module 13.1. [Paste the Greek rubric, then the four baseline reports with their prompts.] Every Rome report was written with Opus 5.5.

Adapt the rubric for Rome. Keep the eight Greek dimensions and their 1 to 5 anchors, and add three: (9) source criticism: the report separates the date of an event from the date of the source that tells it, and treats the late annalists, Livy and Dionysius as derivative where they are; (10) cross-reference integrity: where the prompt points back to a Greek module, the report points back instead of repeating it and does not contradict it; (11) names and forms: Latin names for Roman gods, standard English forms for people and places, BC and AD used consistently.

For each new dimension give a 1 to 5 scale with a concrete description of what a 1, 3 and 5 look like. Then score two Rome reports as calibration samples [paste Rome 0.1 and 1.4 with their prompts] on all eleven dimensions, so later audits are comparable. Output the adapted rubric as a table, the calibration scores as a second table, and a blank scorecard template I can reuse for module 14.2.
report coming soon
14.2

Audit one report

Run once per report. Produces a scorecard, a verdict and an errata table.

Using the Rome rubric below [paste the rubric from 14.1], audit the report below [paste the prompt it answers, then the report]. Work through these steps and show the results of each.

1. Coverage: list every item the prompt names and mark it covered, thin or missing.
2. Fact-check: extract the 15 most checkable claims (dates, names, attributions, numbers, book and chapter citations) and verify each. Mark it correct, wrong (give the correction and the source) or unverifiable. Check every citation you can.
3. Source criticism: for each claim about the regal period or the early Republic, say whether the report makes clear how late its source is.
4. Disputes: for each red-box disagreement, say whether the dispute is real and fairly stated with named sources or scholars on each side. List any important dispute the report leaves out.
5. Cross-references: for every Greek module the prompt points to, list any material the report repeats from it and any contradiction with it.
6. Depth: name the three strongest and the three weakest passages and say why.
7. Self-test: answer each question yourself from the report alone. Flag any model answer that is wrong or ambiguous, and any question answerable without reading the report.
8. Style and structure: body word count against the target, British spelling, preamble or closing filler, mannered phrasing, broken markup.
9. Score every rubric dimension 1 to 5 with a one-line justification.

Verdict: pass (no dimension below 4 and no factual errors), revise (a list of specific edits fixes it) or rewrite (two or more dimensions below 3, or more than two factual errors). End with an errata table: location, current text, corrected text, source.
report coming soon
14.3

Overlap check: deep dives and Greek cross-references

A deep dive has to go deeper, and a cross-reference must not repeat what Greece already said.

Below are an earlier module and the later module that extends or overlaps it. [Paste both, with their prompts.]

The deep-dive pairings are: 3.1 and 4.5 with 11.1; 5.2 to 5.5 with 11.2 and 11.3; 9.3 with 11.4; 5.3 and 5.6 with 11.5; 5.4 with 11.6 and 11.7; the whole of unit 5 with 11.8; 6.3 with 12.1, 12.2, 12.3, 12.5, 12.6 and 12.7; 1.3 with 12.2, 12.3 and 12.6; 5.5 with 12.1, 12.3 and 12.6; 4.1 with 12.2 and 12.4; 5.6 with 12.6; 4.5 and 5.6 with 13.1 to 13.3; 5.4, 7.1, 7.2 and 10.2 with 13.4; 7.1 and 10.2 with 13.5; 7.2 with 13.6; 7.3 with 13.7 and 13.8; 12.6 with 13.2; and inside deep dive III, 13.1 with 13.3 and 13.4, 13.2 with 13.6, and 13.6 and 13.7 with 13.8. The cross-course pairings are: Greek 7.3 with Rome 3.5, 4.4 and 4.5; Greek 7.4 with Rome 7.3, 7.5, 8.4, 13.3 and 13.7; Greek 2.2 with Rome 2.2; Greek 10.12 with Rome 1.3 and 12.8; Greek 4.3 with Rome 12.2; Greek 4.2 and 10.6 with Rome 12.3; Greek 4.4 and 10.7 with Rome 12.5; Greek 10.9, 12.6 and 12.7 with Rome 12.6; Greek 9.3 with Rome 10.4; Greek 0.1 and 3.7 with Rome 10.1; Greek 9.2 with Rome 9.5; Greek 7.2 with Rome 13.1 to 13.3; Greek 12.5 to 12.8 with Rome 13.1 to 13.6. The core pairings, where two survey modules share ground, are: 0.3 with 1.5; 1.3 with 3.5; 2.5 with 3.3; 3.4 and 6.4 with 9.2; 7.4 with 9.1; 5.3 with 9.3; 6.2 with 10.3; 2.3 with 4.2; and 1.6 with 4.1.

Go through the later module section by section and classify each as new material, deeper treatment of a point the earlier module made (say exactly what is added), or repetition. Estimate the share of the module in each class. List every contradiction between the two reports and say which one is right, with the source. Verdict: a deep dive earns its place if at least 70 percent is new or deeper and there are no unresolved contradictions; a cross-course module passes if under 15 percent repeats the Greek module. If it fails, name the sections to cut and what should replace them.

14.6

Review the syllabus itself

The prompts were drafted with Opus 5.5 too. Check them before they generate anything more; this one can run first.

Below is the Rome syllabus, drafted with Opus 5.5 [paste every unit rationale, module title, why line and prompt], and the Greek syllabus it follows [paste the same]. Review it as the course's designer would, and show the results of each step.

1. Structure: does the unit order keep history and myth interleaved with the sources first? Is anything major missing, duplicated or in the wrong unit? Are the three deep dives the right ones?

COURSE A · Brand + AI · IN PROGRESS

Building a Marketable Personal Brand

Not "how to be an influencer": who pays, for what, how long it takes, what it costs in hours, and what the record-as-brand changes about all of it. Nine core units and two deep dives.

3of 11 units written
27%of the course
1audit run
B3next unit to write
ContinueStart the courseA2 Audience and platform economicsBriefsEvery unit's brief, copyable

What you will be able to do

By the end of the course the reader can state, in one sentence, who his audience is and what they pay for; run a publishing system that holds a bi-weekly cadence inside 5–7 h/week for twelve months; and read his own channel metrics against sourced benchmarks to decide, with evidence, whether to continue, change format or stop.

  • Outcome 1 — a positioning a stranger can repeat, tested against five sourced comparables (A1, DD-A1).
  • Outcome 2 — a publishing system that holds bi-weekly for twelve months inside the hour budget (A3, A5, A8).
  • Outcome 3 — a metrics sheet and benchmark table he reads quarterly to decide continue / change format / stop (A6, A9, A7, DD-A2).

Not content for its own sake: every unit is sourced to peer-reviewed, platform-official or large-sample data, labels anecdote and vendor data as such, and ends with a shipped deliverable.

Units, in writing order

A1Positioning: the journey as a formatThe one sentence a stranger can repeat; the journey template (consumed → syllabus → process → record → artefact); tested against vlog ep. 1–2 and five creators who built a brand on a method rather than a topic.soon
A2Audience and platform economicsWho the 30–45 restless-brain audience is, where they are, what converts them; why YouTube long-form compounds; where the written archive lives.
A3The journey systemHow a journey becomes content: capture during (dictated takes, journal, Zepp), recap after, one long-form per milestone, three posts a week from the same material. Season structure: Athens → HM → history → next.soon
A4Voice, disclosure and the recordHonest on camera without a confessional channel. ADHD is public; the line for everything else. How the record stays truthful so it is usable as evidence later.soon
A5Production craftFilming inside a training session, audio, thumbnails in the Shōmen stylesheet, titles that are not clickbait, editing in under 4 h.soon
A6How growth works, per platformYouTube: search vs browse, retention and CTR, why long-form compounds. Instagram: reach is rented, Reels discover, feed serves followers. Substack/own site: slow but owned.
A7Monetisation through ShōmenBrand → commerce without the channel becoming an advert. Product placement that is just him wearing the gi. Sponsorship stories as episodes.soon
A8Legal, tax, timePortuguese side-income rules, Infios contract constraints on public activity, image rights of training partners and minors, time budget against the rebuild.soon
A9What to expect: benchmarksRealistic numbers for a one-person, bi-weekly channel: 0→1k, 1k→10k, 10k→50k, months to each, share of followers who ever buy, the plateau and why most quit there.
DD-A1Deep dive: five creators who did thisWorking-athlete and masters-athlete channels, learning-in-public channels, and ADHD-with-achievement creators that converted into a product or a living. Year one, what they stopped doing.soon
DD-A2Deep dive: the exit mathWhat income, over how many months, would make a non-9-to-5 life survivable for a sole earner. A floor calculation; written last.soon

Audits run

1 · next after three more units (Audit 2 after B3, B4, A1)
  • 2026-10-28 · Audit 1 (Fable 5.1, stratified sample, then full audit). Units: A2·A6·A9 (the audience-growth research) and B1. B1: PASS, 7 of 7 sampled claims hold, deliverable present. A2·A6·A9: sample 1 of 8 FAIL → full audit: 91 claims, 23 FAIL, 0 STALE, nothing invented, no conclusion flips → REWRITE, because the three deliverables (audience profile and allocation; playbook and metrics sheet; benchmarks table and checkpoints) do not exist and two table rows rest on figures no vidIQ page contains. Pattern: qualifiers the source does not carry. One non-accuracy item: a cited creator profile is a minor's; the name comes out. Consequences: B2 waits for the rewrite; every later Brand + AI unit gets a fresh checker (method §1 override). Report: audits/brand-ai/audit-1.html. The three fresh checks that followed (A9 2 errors, 15 should-fix, 8 minor; A2 3/13/11; A6 1/9/7; all applied) are filed as audits/brand-ai/fresh-check-A9.md, fresh-check-A2.md and fresh-check-A6.md; B2's (3 errors, 10 should-fix, 14 minor) as fresh-check-B2.md.

Frame

The brand is the method, and the method is the record

Working name to be decided. "I get consumed by something, build a syllabus, go on the journey, and record it so I don't forget it." Karate was season one. The half marathon, the weight loss and the history course are the next journeys; building with AI is the fourth. Plurality is the register, not the hook: each piece leads with one journey and the breadth shows across pieces. ADHD is public under his name (decided 27 Oct 2026); everything else gets a pre-mortem before first disclosure. Failures stay in the record.

Pick

Journeys, recorded. Shōmen is one path off it, not the engine. Leaving corporate is not on the table for 24 months; this is a build alongside Infios, measured quarterly.

  • Generalist brands die on variety and live on a repeatable format.
  • The ADHD-creator space sells relatability; this has rigour.
  • Every existing output already fits: vlog, syllabi, journal, pcblueprint.

Override if: after six pieces in the format nobody can describe the channel back to him in one sentence. Then the format is wrong, not the breadth.

  • Cost: 5–7 h/week; €0–300 in gear; an editor only after the cadence has held for six months.
  • Horizon: 18–36 months to 1,000 YouTube subscribers at a bi-weekly cadence; a brand that moves Shōmen revenue is a 24-month question at best.
  • Where the research pushes back: the data says season one is one journey in one format, and karate is the thin niche where a 10–20K channel is visible. History and AI are a year-two layer by the format-drift evidence. His call.
