PERSONAL BRAND · HOW IT IS BUILT

How the course gets made

Method, model rules, and the pages that hold the briefs, the audits and the sources. Nothing here is course content.

Now in Academy

Since v7 (28 Oct 2026, A3) the two courses are read here, in Academy, with the history courses; the standalone hub is gone. Where the procedure below and the Project's method.md differ, method.md governs: the Project, the vault path, the audit cadence and the review model are set there. The text below is kept as the v5.1 record.

Build pages

MHow we buildprocedure, models, review rule1·2Build order with models17 units to goP·ABriefs · course A8 openAUAudits and audit promptsnext after three more units (Audit 2 after B3, B4, A1)BIBBibliography140 sourcesLOGChangelogv7.2

Pick

B1 first, written by Opus 5.5 in a new chat in a new Project.

  • B1 makes every later brief cheaper; it is the cheapest unit.
  • A separate Project keeps 21 units out of the personal assistant.
  • Fable reads nothing until four units exist.

Override if: the brand course should move first; then A1 first, B1 second, same setup.

Procedure

  1. Make a Project ("Courses — Brand & AI"). Add to its knowledge: this page, the research report, the five notes files. Project instructions, three lines: who the reader is; this page holds every brief; each unit is written in full, not described.
  2. One chat per unit. Copy the unit's brief from its Briefs page, paste it into a new chat in that Project. Never reuse a chat across units; leaked context is how a course starts repeating itself.
  3. Save the output to the vault as courses/brand-ai/<code>-<slug>.md (e.g. B1-mental-model.md), same shape as the history course. Hand it to the assistant: it commits, adds the unit to Learn, moves the counters, adds a changelog line.
  4. Units that need live facts (A8, B2, B8, DD-A1, DD-B3) are written with web search on; the briefs say so. Everything else without.
  5. After every fourth unit, run the audit prompt from the Audits page. The first fires after B1.

Models

  • Writing: Opus 5.5 for all remaining units. The quality gap shows most on long-form synthesis, which is this.
  • The shipped deliverable is written in the same chat as its unit, never separately, so it inherits the unit's reasoning.
  • Reviewing: Fable 5.1, in the personal-assistant Project where this hub lives. One claim per section per unit checked against its source; more than one in ten fails → full audit.
  • Not Sonnet or Haiku for units or audits. They are the right tools for turning a finished unit into posts, a Shorts list or an email; that is B7's pipeline.

Review rule

After every four written units, across both courses, Fable 5.1 audits by stratified sample: one claim per section per unit, checked against its source; more than one in ten fails → full audit of that unit. Units written with live search (A2, A6, A9, A8, B2, B8, DD-B3) are re-checked for staleness six months after writing.

Audit 1 ran 28 Oct: B1 PASS; A2·A6·A9 REWRITE. The three were rewritten the same day from their briefs, each fresh-checked by Fable 5.1 (A9 2 errors, A2 3, A6 1; all fixed). B2 is next; every unit gets a fresh checker.

Academy›Course A · Personal brand›Build order
PERSONAL BRAND · BUILD ORDER

Building order, with models

Seventeen units remain. One row per unit in writing order; an audit row every fourth written unit. Opus 5.5 writes; Fable 5.1 audits and writes the last deep dive, which needs the chat history. "Search" means the unit is written with web search on because its facts are current.

  • Why this order: B1–B4 first so every later brief is cheaper; A1 and A3 next because the Athens edit and the half-marathon season need the positioning and the journey system now; DD-B3 between them (added 28 Oct 2026) so B5, B7 and DD-B1 inherit the practitioners' methods it verifies; B5 beside A3 and B7 beside A5 because they build the same pipeline; A7, A8, B6, B8 once there is a channel to measure; deep dives last, DD-B2 last of all.
  • Cadence: one unit per 3–4 days is the sustainable rate alongside the half-marathon block; that is 10–11 weeks to the end, with audits adding an evening each.
  • Audit gate: no new unit starts while an audit verdict is REWRITE on an earlier one.

Academy›Course A · Personal brand›Briefs
PERSONAL BRAND · BRIEFS

Building a Marketable Personal Brand: the briefs

One brief per unit, self-contained: the shared preamble (reader, brand thesis, course goal, sourcing standard, tone, citation rules) is baked into every open brief. Copy, paste into a new Opus chat, one unit per chat. Written units show their note instead. The course itself is read in Course A.

A1

Positioning: the journey as a format

not written

The one sentence a stranger can repeat; the journey template (consumed → syllabus → process → record → artefact); tested against vlog ep. 1–2 and five creators who built a brand on a method rather than a topic.

Shipspositioning statement + channel rename + description

Opus brief
Write one unit of a self-study course in the same long-form report format as his history course: a long-form report for one reader. Length is whatever the topic needs (2,000–6,000 words); do not pad and do not cut to a target. Reader: Pedro, 35, Porto. Senior Director of Product Management (UX/AI) at a PE-backed software company; ADHD; ended competitive karate after the WIKF Global Cup (Oct 2026), still trains Wado-Ryū and kumite and coaches; next journeys are a sub-1:45 half marathon in spring/summer 2027, −15 kg, and a self-taught history course; owns Shōmen, a small karate-apparel brand; publishes in English with some Portuguese; has 5–7 h/week. Brand thesis (working name to be decided) — he gets consumed by a journey, builds a syllabus, records the process so he does not forget it, and ships the artefact; plurality is the register, each piece leads with one journey; ADHD is public under his name; failures stay in the record. Tone: direct, no flattery, no filler, no motivational language, no 'not X but Y' constructions. Course goal: by the end of the course the reader can state, in one sentence, who his audience is and what they pay for; run a publishing system that holds a bi-weekly cadence inside 5–7 h/week for twelve months; and read his own channel metrics against sourced benchmarks to decide, with evidence, whether to continue, change format or stop. Sourcing standard: every claim that carries a number, a mechanism or a recommendation is sourced; acceptable sources are peer-reviewed work, platform-official documentation or statements, large-sample vendor datasets (named, with sample size and date), and documented creator cases with verifiable numbers; creator anecdotes are labelled as such; correlational and vendor data is labelled as such; no uncited figures; no invented handles, names or numbers; if a claim cannot be sourced, say so and leave it out of the deliverable. A unit that reads as advice without evidence is a failed unit. Where the unit depends on current facts (prices, platform rules, model lineups), search before writing and date the facts. End with the unit's deliverable, built out in full, not described.

Unit A1, Positioning. Cover: what a positioning is and is not (a sentence a stranger can repeat vs a description of the person); why method-based brands survive variety and topic-based ones do not; the journey template as the recognisable format; three candidate umbrella names for the brand with the case for and against each; five verified creators whose brand is a method (name, platform, numbers, what their one sentence is); how the two existing vlog episodes ('Sometimes You Win, Sometimes You Learn. Mostly It Sucks'; 'Being a Better Athlete Made Me a Worse Person') already express or contradict the positioning. Deliverable: the positioning statement (one sentence plus a 60-word expansion), three channel-name options with one recommended, and a YouTube channel description and Instagram bio written in full.

Deliverable: the journey template as a reusable checklist, and a 12-week calendar from 3 Nov 2026 covering the Athens edit, the HM season opener and the weekly email, with hours per item.

Deliverable: a production checklist (pre, during, post) and an edit template with timings.

Academy›Course A · Personal brand›Audits
PERSONAL BRAND · AUDITS

Fable audit log and prompts

One audit run. Each audit records: units sampled, claims checked, failures, verdict (pass / full audit / rewrite), and what changed.

Audit log

2026-10-28Audit 1 (Fable 5.1, stratified sample, then full audit). Units: A2·A6·A9 (the audience-growth research) and B1. B1: PASS, 7 of 7 sampled claims hold, deliverable present. A2·A6·A9: sample 1 of 8 FAIL → full audit: 91 claims, 23 FAIL, 0 STALE, nothing invented, no conclusion flips → REWRITE, because the three deliverables (audience profile and allocation; playbook and metrics sheet; benchmarks table and checkpoints) do not exist and two table rows rest on figures no vidIQ page contains. Pattern: qualifiers the source does not carry. One non-accuracy item: a cited creator profile is a minor's; the name comes out. Consequences: B2 waits for the rewrite; every later Brand + AI unit gets a fresh checker (method §1 override). Report: audits/brand-ai/audit-1.html. The three fresh checks that followed (A9 2 errors, 15 should-fix, 8 minor; A2 3/13/11; A6 1/9/7; all applied) are filed as audits/brand-ai/fresh-check-A9.md, fresh-check-A2.md and fresh-check-A6.md; B2's (3 errors, 10 should-fix, 14 minor) as fresh-check-B2.md.

Audit prompts

Run in the personal-assistant Project with Fable 5.1, on command, after every fourth written unit. Paste the units or point at their vault paths.

Stratified-sample audit (every four units)
You are auditing four units of a self-study course written by Opus 5.5 for one reader. Units: {list the four unit codes and their vault paths}.

Method, in this order:
1. For each unit, list its sections. From every section draw one claim that carries a number, a named source, a mechanism or a recommendation. That is the sample; record it before checking anything.
2. Check each sampled claim against the source the unit cites: open the source; confirm the figure, the date, the sample size and that the claim is what the source says, not a stretch of it. Mark each claim PASS, FAIL (wrong, unsupported, miscited, or invented) or STALE (true when written, superseded now, with the newer figure).
3. For each unit compute the failure rate. If more than one claim in ten is FAIL, audit that unit in full: every cited claim, same procedure.
4. Check the deliverable: it exists in full inside the unit, it follows from the unit's evidence, and it is usable without reading the unit.
5. Check against the course goal and sourcing standard (in the unit's preamble): does the unit move the reader toward the stated outcomes, and is anything in it advice without evidence?

Output: a table per unit (section, claim, source, verdict, note); the failure rate; a verdict per unit (PASS / FULL AUDIT RUN, result / REWRITE with the sections named); the three most consequential errors across the four units in plain language; and one line each for the bibliography (new sources to add, sources to mark failed) and the changelog. Do not fix the units; report.
Staleness check (six months after a live-search unit)
You are re-checking one unit written with live search six months ago: {unit code, vault path, date written}. Search again, today, for every fact in it that can change: platform rules and thresholds, prices and plan limits, model lineups, benchmark figures, regulatory status, the numbers attached to named creators. For each: the value in the unit, the value now, the source and date for the new value, and whether the unit's conclusion still holds. End with a verdict: CURRENT / PATCH (list the lines to change) / REWRITE (say which sections). Do not rewrite; report.

Academy›Technology›Course B · Building with AI
COURSE B · Brand + AI · IN PROGRESS

Using AI to Build a Brand

Not how LLMs work. Which model for which of his tasks, how to brief so he stops re-explaining, where the project setup leaks, and what he does not know he is doing badly. Eight core units and three deep dives.

2of 11 units written
18%of the course
1audit run
B3next unit to write
ContinueStart the courseB1 Mental modelBriefsEvery unit's brief, copyable

What you will be able to do

By the end of the course the reader routes each recurring task to the right model and effort level without guessing; briefs any unit, draft or audit so that the first output is usable; keeps a project structure that does not rot; and verifies AI-written material by sampling before trusting it.

  • Outcome 1 — a model-routing table for every recurring task, with cost and effort (B1, B2).
  • Outcome 2 — a prompt library and a project structure that produce usable first outputs (B3, B4, B5, DD-B3).
  • Outcome 3 — a verification protocol and a content pipeline, both run on real work before the course ends (B6, B7, B8, DD-B1).

Not content for its own sake: every unit is sourced to peer-reviewed, platform-official or large-sample data, labels anecdote and vendor data as such, and ends with a shipped deliverable.

Units, in writing order

B1Mental modelContext window, tokens, what a model retains and does not, why 'it forgot' is usually 'it was never told'. Where the memory system fits.B2Choosing the modelThe current lineup: Haiku for triage, Sonnet for drafting, Opus for long-form, Fable for audit. Cost per task, not per token.
B3Prompting that holds upRole, constraints, examples, output contract. Why the preferences file works and one-line asks sometimes don't. Negative examples.next
B4Context engineeringProjects, memory, skills, what goes where. Why file temperatures exist. When to start a new chat.soon
B5Skills and automationHow plugin skills fire, how to write one, scheduled tasks, where the vault and pcblueprint plug in.soon
B6Research and verificationSearch vs deep research vs asking directly. How models fail (confident, specific, wrong). The stratified-sample audit, generalised.soon
B7Content pipelineDictated take → script → titles → thumbnail copy → post variants, model-routed. Where AI helps his voice and where it flattens it.soon
B8Cost, limits, hygienePlan limits, token budgets, what burns usage for no gain. Privacy of what is stored.soon
DD-B1Deep dive: agentsClaude Code, subagents, Strider and AIssistant as cases. What an agent is good for, what it quietly wrecks.soon
DD-B2Deep dive: his unknown unknownsWritten last. A review of 90 days of chats in this project: where he asked badly, where the tool was wrong for the job, where he did by hand what a skill already does.soon
DD-B3Deep dive: how the best practitioners use the toolKarpathy's documented practice 2025–26 (vibe coding, Software 3.0, agent-first coding, autoresearch, the LLM Knowledge Base) and every other method with a named author and a primary source: Anthropic's and OpenAI's own guides, Willison, Mollick, spec-driven workflows, the chain-of-thought literature. What is verified, what is folklore, what transfers from code to his work.soon

Academy›Course B · Building with AI›How it is built
BUILDING WITH AI · HOW IT IS BUILT

How the course gets made

Method, model rules, and the pages that hold the briefs, the audits and the sources. Nothing here is course content.

Now in Academy

Since v7 (28 Oct 2026, A3) the two courses are read here, in Academy, with the history courses; the standalone hub is gone. Where the procedure below and the Project's method.md differ, method.md governs: the Project, the vault path, the audit cadence and the review model are set there. The text below is kept as the v5.1 record.

Build pages

MHow we buildprocedure, models, review rule1·2Build order with models17 units to goP·BBriefs · course B9 openAUAudits and audit promptsnext after three more units (Audit 2 after B3, B4, A1)BIBBibliography58 sourcesLOGChangelogv7.2

Pick

B1 first, written by Opus 5.5 in a new chat in a new Project.

  • B1 makes every later brief cheaper; it is the cheapest unit.
  • A separate Project keeps 21 units out of the personal assistant.
  • Fable reads nothing until four units exist.

Override if: the brand course should move first; then A1 first, B1 second, same setup.

Procedure

  1. Make a Project ("Courses — Brand & AI"). Add to its knowledge: this page, the research report, the five notes files. Project instructions, three lines: who the reader is; this page holds every brief; each unit is written in full, not described.
  2. One chat per unit. Copy the unit's brief from its Briefs page, paste it into a new chat in that Project. Never reuse a chat across units; leaked context is how a course starts repeating itself.
  3. Save the output to the vault as courses/brand-ai/<code>-<slug>.md (e.g. B1-mental-model.md), same shape as the history course. Hand it to the assistant: it commits, adds the unit to Learn, moves the counters, adds a changelog line.
  4. Units that need live facts (A8, B2, B8, DD-A1, DD-B3) are written with web search on; the briefs say so. Everything else without.
  5. After every fourth unit, run the audit prompt from the Audits page. The first fires after B1.

Models

  • Writing: Opus 5.5 for all remaining units. The quality gap shows most on long-form synthesis, which is this.
  • The shipped deliverable is written in the same chat as its unit, never separately, so it inherits the unit's reasoning.
  • Reviewing: Fable 5.1, in the personal-assistant Project where this hub lives. One claim per section per unit checked against its source; more than one in ten fails → full audit.
  • Not Sonnet or Haiku for units or audits. They are the right tools for turning a finished unit into posts, a Shorts list or an email; that is B7's pipeline.

Review rule

After every four written units, across both courses, Fable 5.1 audits by stratified sample: one claim per section per unit, checked against its source; more than one in ten fails → full audit of that unit. Units written with live search (A2, A6, A9, A8, B2, B8, DD-B3) are re-checked for staleness six months after writing.

Audit 1 ran 28 Oct: B1 PASS; A2·A6·A9 REWRITE. The three were rewritten the same day from their briefs, each fresh-checked by Fable 5.1 (A9 2 errors, A2 3, A6 1; all fixed). B2 is next; every unit gets a fresh checker.

Academy›Course B · Building with AI›Build order
BUILDING WITH AI · BUILD ORDER

Building order, with models

Seventeen units remain. One row per unit in writing order; an audit row every fourth written unit. Opus 5.5 writes; Fable 5.1 audits and writes the last deep dive, which needs the chat history. "Search" means the unit is written with web search on because its facts are current.

  • Why this order: B1–B4 first so every later brief is cheaper; A1 and A3 next because the Athens edit and the half-marathon season need the positioning and the journey system now; DD-B3 between them (added 28 Oct 2026) so B5, B7 and DD-B1 inherit the practitioners' methods it verifies; B5 beside A3 and B7 beside A5 because they build the same pipeline; A7, A8, B6, B8 once there is a channel to measure; deep dives last, DD-B2 last of all.
  • Cadence: one unit per 3–4 days is the sustainable rate alongside the half-marathon block; that is 10–11 weeks to the end, with audits adding an evening each.
  • Audit gate: no new unit starts while an audit verdict is REWRITE on an earlier one.

Academy›Course B · Building with AI›Briefs
BUILDING WITH AI · BRIEFS

Using AI to Build a Brand: the briefs

One brief per unit, self-contained: the shared preamble (reader, brand thesis, course goal, sourcing standard, tone, citation rules) is baked into every open brief. Copy, paste into a new Opus chat, one unit per chat. Written units show their note instead. The course itself is read in Course B.

B1

Mental model

written

Context window, tokens, what a model retains and does not, why 'it forgot' is usually 'it was never told'. Where the memory system fits.

Shipsone-page model he can explain in five minutes

Read the unit →
Opus brief
Unit B1, Mental model. Cover: the context window as the single thing he controls; tokens as cost and as attention; what persists within a chat vs across chats (nothing, unless a memory system writes it); training knowledge vs retrieved knowledge vs what is in the conversation; why the same prompt gives different answers; what a system prompt, a project instruction and a user message each do; where his setup (claude.ai Project with memory, custom instructions, plugin skills, scheduled tasks, a GitHub vault, Claude Code occasionally) sits in that picture, used as the worked example throughout. Deliverable: a one-page model of how Claude works that he could explain to his partner in five minutes, as plain prose.

Deep dive B3, How the best practitioners use the tool. Search first and open every primary source; date each fact. 'The Karpathy method' is a label coined by bloggers and newsletters, not by Karpathy; the unit says so and builds only from his own posts, talks, repositories and interviews, discarding any practice a secondary source attributes to him that his own words do not support (one blog cites an 'AISN 2026' talk of which no record can be found; treat such claims as unsourced). Cover, in this order: Karpathy's documented practice and what each piece actually says — the vibe-coding post (Feb 2025) and his later distinction between vibe coding and agentic engineering; the 'How I use LLMs' video (Feb 2025); the Software 3.0 talk at YC AI Startup School (Jun 2025: the autonomy slider, the generation–verification loop, keeping the agent on a leash, the context window as the lever); his endorsement of 'context engineering' over 'prompt engineering' (Jun 2025); the Dwarkesh Patel interview (Oct 2025) and the shift to agent-first coding he described in his own post in Jan 2026 (about 80% manual in November to about 80% agent-driven in December; reviewing as a separate skill from writing; the failure modes he lists); nanochat (Oct 2025) and autoresearch (Mar 2026: the loop, the one file the agent may edit, the five-minute budget, the keep-or-revert rule, the run of about 700 experiments and its reported gain); the LLM Knowledge Bases post (Apr 2026: raw sources → compiled Markdown wiki → lint → query, Markdown as the source of truth the human rarely edits, about 100 articles and 400,000 words navigated by index without a vector store); the Sequoia AI Ascent 2026 talk (verifiability decides what gets automated; delegate thinking, keep understanding); and his move to Anthropic (May 2026) as context for reading his later statements. Then the other methods that have a primary source and a named author, each stated from that source with its date and an evidence grade (measured, documented practice, or stated opinion): Anthropic's 'Building effective agents' (Dec 2024), 'Effective context engineering for AI agents' (Sep 2025) and the Claude Code best-practices post (Apr 2025); OpenAI's 'A practical guide to building agents' (Apr 2025) and its model prompting guides; Simon Willison's definition of vibe coding against AI-assisted programming (Mar 2025), his account of how he uses LLMs to write code (Mar 2025) and the lethal trifecta (Jun 2025); Ethan Mollick's four principles from Co-Intelligence (2024) and the centaur/cyborg finding of the BCG field experiment (Dell'Acqua et al. 2023, with its sample size and where performance fell); spec-driven development as practised (Harper Reed's codegen workflow, Feb 2025; GitHub Spec Kit, Sep 2025; AWS Kiro, Jul 2025); and the prompting literature with its limits (chain-of-thought, Wei et al. 2022; ReAct, Yao et al. 2022; self-consistency, Wang et al. 2022; where chain-of-thought does not help, Sprague et al. 2024). For every method: what it claims, what evidence it rests on, who it was written for, and whether it transfers from code to his work (course units, research, scripts, the vault, the memory system, the skills), decided case by case; where a method is for software only, say so and leave it there. Then what of it he already does without the name, and what collides with his setup as B1, B2 and the units written since describe it. Flag for B4 (the knowledge-base pattern against his vault and Project memory), B5 (a skill file as an outcome description) and DD-B1 (the autonomy slider, verifiability as the delegation test, autoresearch as a loop) what each should take from this unit; DD-B1 keeps ownership of agents. Deliverable: method cards, one per method (author, primary source with date, the claim in one sentence, evidence grade, what he adopts, what he leaves and why), then his operating rules: ten rules he adopts, each traced to a card, written to be pasted into his way-of-working file.
Academy›Course B · Building with AI›Audits
BUILDING WITH AI · AUDITS

Fable audit log and prompts

One audit run. Each audit records: units sampled, claims checked, failures, verdict (pass / full audit / rewrite), and what changed.

Audit log

2026-10-28Audit 1 (Fable 5.1, stratified sample, then full audit). Units: A2·A6·A9 (the audience-growth research) and B1. B1: PASS, 7 of 7 sampled claims hold, deliverable present. A2·A6·A9: sample 1 of 8 FAIL → full audit: 91 claims, 23 FAIL, 0 STALE, nothing invented, no conclusion flips → REWRITE, because the three deliverables (audience profile and allocation; playbook and metrics sheet; benchmarks table and checkpoints) do not exist and two table rows rest on figures no vidIQ page contains. Pattern: qualifiers the source does not carry. One non-accuracy item: a cited creator profile is a minor's; the name comes out. Consequences: B2 waits for the rewrite; every later Brand + AI unit gets a fresh checker (method §1 override). Report: audits/brand-ai/audit-1.html. The three fresh checks that followed (A9 2 errors, 15 should-fix, 8 minor; A2 3/13/11; A6 1/9/7; all applied) are filed as audits/brand-ai/fresh-check-A9.md, fresh-check-A2.md and fresh-check-A6.md; B2's (3 errors, 10 should-fix, 14 minor) as fresh-check-B2.md.

Audit prompts

Run in the personal-assistant Project with Fable 5.1, on command, after every fourth written unit. Paste the units or point at their vault paths.

Stratified-sample audit (every four units)
You are auditing four units of a self-study course written by Opus 5.5 for one reader. Units: {list the four unit codes and their vault paths}.

Method, in this order:
1. For each unit, list its sections. From every section draw one claim that carries a number, a named source, a mechanism or a recommendation. That is the sample; record it before checking anything.
2. Check each sampled claim against the source the unit cites: open the source; confirm the figure, the date, the sample size and that the claim is what the source says, not a stretch of it. Mark each claim PASS, FAIL (wrong, unsupported, miscited, or invented) or STALE (true when written, superseded now, with the newer figure).
3. For each unit compute the failure rate. If more than one claim in ten is FAIL, audit that unit in full: every cited claim, same procedure.
4. Check the deliverable: it exists in full inside the unit, it follows from the unit's evidence, and it is usable without reading the unit.
5. Check against the course goal and sourcing standard (in the unit's preamble): does the unit move the reader toward the stated outcomes, and is anything in it advice without evidence?

Output: a table per unit (section, claim, source, verdict, note); the failure rate; a verdict per unit (PASS / FULL AUDIT RUN, result / REWRITE with the sections named); the three most consequential errors across the four units in plain language; and one line each for the bibliography (new sources to add, sources to mark failed) and the changelog. Do not fix the units; report.
Staleness check (six months after a live-search unit)
You are re-checking one unit written with live search six months ago: {unit code, vault path, date written}. Search again, today, for every fact in it that can change: platform rules and thresholds, prices and plan limits, model lineups, benchmark figures, regulatory status, the numbers attached to named creators. For each: the value in the unit, the value now, the source and date for the new value, and whether the unit's conclusion still holds. End with a verdict: CURRENT / PATCH (list the lines to change) / REWRITE (say which sections). Do not rewrite; report.
